<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We classify all equality cases in the sharp one-dimensional Lieb–Thirring inequality for every finite matrix size and $`1/2\lt \gamma\lt 3/2`$. For measurable Hermitian positive semidefinite potentials <i>W</i> with $`\int_\mathbb R\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits (W^{\gamma+1/2})\lt \infty`$, equality holds precisely for direct sums, in one constant unitary basis, of scalar one-bound-state solitons and zero channels. The nonzero solitons may have independent scales and centers.

- **claim-equality-classification** (classification, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): For every $W$ satisfying <a href="#intro:class" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="intro:class">[intro:class]</a>, $$\label{intro:sharp} \mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(H_W)_-^\gamma\le C_\gamma\int_\mathbb R\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(W^p), \qquad C_\gamma=\left(\frac{\gamma-1/2}{\gamma+1/2}\right)^{\gamma-1/2} \frac{\Gamma(\gamma+1)}{\sqrt\pi\,\Gamma(\gamma+3/2)}.$$ Put $r=(\gamma-1/2)^{-1}$. Equality holds if and only if there are $k\in\{0,\ldots,m\}$, a constant unitary matrix $U$, positive numbers $a_1,\ldots,a_k$, and real numbers $x_1,\ldots,x_k$ such that almost everywhere $$\label{intro:classification} W(x)=U\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}\bigl(w_1(x),\ldots,w_k(x),0,\ldots,0\bigr)U^*, \quad w_j(x)=(r+1)a_j^2\mathop{\mathrm{sech}}^2\!\bigl(ra_j(x-x_j)\bigr).$$ The case $k=0$ means $W=0$. Each nonzero channel has exactly one negative eigenvalue, $-a_j^2$, and therefore every extremal potential has at most $m$ negative eigenvalues, counted with multiplicity.
  - Explanation: Characterizes all equality cases of the sharp one-dimensional matrix Lieb-Thirring inequality for the open range of the exponent as direct sums, in one fixed unitary basis, of scalar hyperbolic-secant solitons and zero channels with independent scales and centers.
- **claim-spectral-form** (theorem, [source](#stmt-pre-spectral)): Let $p>1$ and let $W:\mathbb R\to\mathbb C^{m\times m}$ be measurable, Hermitian and positive semidefinite, with $\int_\mathbb R\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(W^p)<\infty$. The form $h_W$ on $H^1(\mathbb R;\mathbb C^m)$ is closed and bounded below. Multiplication by $W^{1/2}$ is compact from $H^1$ to $L^2$. The negative spectrum of $H_W$ consists of isolated eigenvalues of finite multiplicity, with possible accumulation only at zero. Its eigenfunctions belong to $H^1$ and satisfy $-\psi''-W\psi=\lambda\psi$ distributionally.
  - Explanation: For an integrable positive semidefinite matrix potential the quadratic form is closed and bounded below, and the negative spectrum is made of isolated eigenvalues of finite multiplicity accumulating at most at zero.
- **claim-trace-young** (theorem, [source](#stmt-pre-young)): For positive semidefinite matrices $W,Z$ of the same finite size, set $$\label{pre:young-defect} Y(W,Z)=D_\sigma\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(W^p)-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(WZ)+\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(Z^q).$$ Then $Y(W,Z)\ge0$, with equality if and only if $W=qZ^r$. Also, $$\label{pre:young-coercive} Y(W,Z)\ge\tfrac12\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(Z^q) -(2^{p-1}-1)D_\sigma\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(W^p).$$
  - Explanation: The trace Young defect is nonnegative and vanishes exactly when the potential equals a fixed multiple of a power of the density matrix; a coercive lower bound is also given.
- **claim-quantitative-action** (bound, [source](#stmt-act-quantitative-action)): Let $0<\sigma<1$ and $\kappa>0$. There is a constant $c_*=c_*(\sigma,\kappa)>0$ with the following property. For $n,d,\Omega,K$ as above, suppose $U\in H^1_0(\Omega;\mathbb R^{n\times d})$ satisfies $$\int_\Omega UU^{\mathsf T}\,dx=I_n.$$ For every $\eta>0$ there exist a real lower triangular matrix $C\in\mathbb R^{n\times n}$ and a path $B\in C^1(\overline\Omega;\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_n)$ such that $$B(x_-)=K,\qquad B(x_+)=-K,\qquad \sup_{x\in\overline\Omega}\\|B(x)\\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}\leq\kappa,$$ and, for $A=CU$, $$\label{act:bound} \mathcal E_K(A)\geq b_\sigma\sum_{i=1}^n\ell_i^{2\gamma} +\int_\Omega\left(\\|A'-BA\\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2 +c_*\Phi_K(A,B)\right)\,dx-\eta.$$ In particular, $c_*$ is independent of $n,d,\Omega,U$, and the smallest diagonal entry of $K$.
  - Explanation: A finite-interval lower bound for the action that keeps the first-order square and an algebraic defect, with a coefficient independent of the number of rows.
- **claim-constraint-coercivity** (bound, [source](#stmt-act-constraint)): The constant $c_*(\sigma,\kappa)>0$ can be chosen so that, whenever $0<\delta\leq1$, $\\|B\\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}\leq\kappa$, and $M_\delta(B)=AA^{\mathsf T}$, one has $$\label{act:constraint-bound} F(A,B)+c_*\Phi_K(A,B)\leq E_{n,\delta}, \qquad E_{n,\delta} =n\bigl(\delta\kappa^{2\sigma} +\delta^\sigma(\kappa^2+\delta)\bigr) +\delta^{2\sigma}.$$
  - Explanation: On the regularized field constraint, the nonlinear term plus a multiple of the algebraic defect is bounded by an explicit small error.
- **claim-sharp-bound** (bound, [source](#stmt-bound-sharp)): Every potential in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> satisfies $$\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(H_W)_-^\gamma\le\frac{D_\sigma}{b_\sigma} \int_\mathbb R\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(W^p).$$
  - Explanation: Recovers the sharp spectral moment inequality for every potential of the main theorem from the action estimate.
- **claim-pointwise-relations** (theorem, [source](#stmt-comp-relations)): Under these assumptions, there are rows $a_i\in H^1(\mathbb R;\mathbb R^m)$, $i\in\mathcal I$, each a linear combination of eigenfunctions with eigenvalue $-k_i^2$, such that $$-a_i''-a_iW=-k_i^2a_i \quad\text{in distributions.} \label{comp:row-eigenfunction}$$ The rows $a_i$ are allowed to vanish. On a common set of full measure, they define a Hilbert–Schmidt operator $A(x):\mathbb R^m\to\mathscr H$, and there exists a bounded self-adjoint operator $B$ on $\mathscr H$ with $\\|B\\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}\leq\kappa$ such that $$=0,\qquad \Lambda^2-B^2=(AA^T)^r,\qquad a_i'(x)=(BA)_{i,\cdot}\quad(i\in\mathcal I). \label{comp:limit-relations}$$ Moreover, $$W(x)=q\bigl(A(x)^TA(x)\bigr)^r \quad\text{almost everywhere.} \label{comp:potential-limit}$$ The operator $B$ is asserted to exist separately at each such point $x$; no measurable choice is required.
  - Explanation: At equality the eigenfunction rows satisfy exact pointwise algebraic and differential relations with a bounded self-adjoint operator, and the potential is a power of the density.
- **claim-fixed-right-spaces** (theorem, [source](#stmt-rig-fixed-right-spaces)): For every $\alpha$, the subspace $$\mathcal R_\alpha=(\ker G_\alpha(x))^\perp\subset\mathbb R^m$$ is independent of $x\in\mathbb R$. The nonzero spaces $\mathcal R_\alpha$ are mutually orthogonal, and $$\label{rig:dimension-bound} \sum_\alpha\dim\mathcal R_\alpha\le m.$$ In particular, only finitely many $G_\alpha$ are nonzero. The potential $W$ has a continuous representative, equal to zero on the orthogonal complement of these spaces and equal to $q(G_\alpha^TG_\alpha)^r$ on $\mathcal R_\alpha$. With this representative, every $G_\alpha$ is $C^2$.
  - Explanation: The row spaces of the blocks are constant in position, mutually orthogonal, at most m in total dimension, and the potential has a continuous representative.
- **claim-fixed-left-spaces** (theorem, [source](#stmt-rig-fixed-left-spaces)): For each $\alpha$ with $\ell:=\dim\mathcal R_\alpha>0$, the range of $G_\alpha(x)$ in $\mathbb R^{n_\alpha}$ is independent of $x$. In constant orthonormal bases of this range and of $\mathcal R_\alpha$, the restriction of $G_\alpha$ is an invertible $C^2$ matrix $D:\mathbb R\to\mathbb R^{\ell\times\ell}$. The matrix $B_0=D'D^{-1}$ is $C^1$ and satisfies, everywhere on $\mathbb R$, $$\label{rig:active-identities} B_0=B_0^T,\qquad \alpha^2I-B_0^2=(DD^T)^r>0,\qquad B_0'=r(B_0^2-\alpha^2I).$$
  - Explanation: The range of each nonzero block is constant, reducing to an invertible matrix function whose logarithmic derivative solves a matrix Riccati equation.
- **claim-trace-deficit** (theorem, [source](#stmt-trace-deficit)): Let $n\geq1$ and let $B,V\in\mathbb C^{n\times n}$ be Hermitian. Suppose that $$\label{trace:symbol} Y_s=(sI-B)^2,\qquad X_s=Y_s+V\geq bI \quad(s\in\mathbb R)$$ for some $b>0$. Set $$\label{trace:resolvents} R(t)=\frac1\pi\int_\mathbb R(X_s+tI)^{-1}\,ds, \qquad P(t)=t^{-1/2}I-R(t),\qquad t>0,$$ and $$\label{trace:N} N=c_\sigma\int_0^\infty t^\beta P(t)\,dt.$$ These integrals converge absolutely in matrix norm, and $R(t)>0$. There are continuous Hermitian matrices $L(t)=R(t)^{-1}>0$ and $Z(t)$ satisfying $$\label{trace:LZ} V+tI=L^2+Z^2+i[B,Z],\qquad LZ+ZL+i[B,L]=0.$$ If $N\geq0$, then $$\label{trace:deficit-identity} \begin{split} \mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(NV)-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(N^q) =c_\sigma\int_0^\infty t^\beta\Bigl( \mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(RZ^2) +q\bigl\\|R^{-1/2}-R^{1/2}(tI+N^r)^{1/2}\bigr\\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2 \Bigr)\,dt. \end{split}$$ In particular, $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(NV)\geq\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(N^q)$.
  - Explanation: An exact integral identity writes the trace deficit as an integral of two nonnegative terms.
- **claim-field-properties** (construction, [source](#stmt-field-properties)): The limit in <a href="#field:definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="field:definition">[field:definition]</a> exists locally uniformly, and $M_\delta:\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_n\to\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_n$ is locally Lipschitz. There is a $C^1$ function $J_\delta:\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_n\to\mathbb R$, normalized by $J_\delta(0)=0$, such that $$\label{field:primitive} dJ_\delta(B)[H]=-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(M_\delta(B)H).$$ The scalar function $\mu_\delta$ is locally Lipschitz and strictly increasing, with $\mu_\delta(0)=0$, and $$\label{field:mu-positive} \mu_\delta(z)=(z+\delta)^\sigma-\delta^\sigma \qquad(z\geq0).$$ For every real unit vector $e$, and for every standard basis vector $e_i$, respectively, $$\begin{aligned} e^{\mathsf T}M_\delta(B)e &\leq\mu_\delta\bigl(e^{\mathsf T}K^2e -(e^{\mathsf T}Be)^2\bigr), \label{field:direction}\\ Be_i=ye_i\quad&\Longrightarrow\quad M_\delta(B)e_i=\mu_\delta(k_i^2-y^2)e_i. \label{field:coordinate} \end{aligned}$$ For each diagonal sign matrix $S$, $$\label{field:equivariance} M_\delta(SBS)=SM_\delta(B)S.$$ Finally, $$\label{field:endpoints} \begin{split} J_\delta(-K)-J_\delta(K) &=\sum_{i=1}^n\int_{-k_i}^{k_i}\mu_\delta(k_i^2-s^2)\,ds\\ &\longrightarrow b_\sigma\sum_{i=1}^n k_i^{2\sigma+1} \qquad(\delta\downarrow0). \end{split}$$
  - Explanation: The regularized matrix field exists, is locally Lipschitz, has a primitive, and satisfies directional, coordinate and equivariance relations with an explicit endpoint limit.
- **claim-field-positive** (theorem, [source](#stmt-field-positive)): If $M=M_\delta(B)\geq0$, then $$\label{field:positive-bounds} Q_s(B)\geq0\quad(s\in\mathbb R),\qquad 0\leq M\leq\kappa^{2\sigma}I.$$ Set $V=K^2-B^2+\delta I$ and use $B,V$ in <a href="#trace:resolvents" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="trace:resolvents">[trace:resolvents]</a> and <a href="#trace:N" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="trace:N">[trace:N]</a>. Then the symbol $(sI-B)^2+V=Q_s(B)+\delta I$ is bounded below by $\delta I$, and $$\label{field:N} N=M+\delta^\sigma I.$$ In particular, the hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#trace:deficit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="trace:deficit">10</a> hold.
  - Explanation: Positive values of the field force positivity of the quadratic symbol and put the situation within the hypotheses of the trace deficit theorem.
- **claim-connecting-path** (construction, [source](#stmt-path-connecting)): Let $\Omega=(x_-,x_+)$ be bounded and let $U\in H^1_0(\Omega;\mathbb R^{n\times d})$, where $n,d\geq1$, satisfy $$\int_\Omega UU^{\mathsf T}\,dx=I_n.$$ For every $\delta,\varepsilon>0$ there are $C_\varepsilon\in\mathcal L_n$ and $B_\varepsilon\in C^1(\overline\Omega;\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_n)$ such that, with $A_\varepsilon=C_\varepsilon U$, $$\label{path:ode} \varepsilon B_\varepsilon' =M_\delta(B_\varepsilon)-A_\varepsilon A_\varepsilon^{\mathsf T}, \qquad B_\varepsilon(x_-)=K,\quad B_\varepsilon(x_+)=-K.$$ They satisfy $$\label{path:bounds} \sup_{x\in\overline\Omega}\\|B_\varepsilon(x)\\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}\leq\kappa, \qquad \sup_{0<\varepsilon\leq1}\\|C_\varepsilon\\|_{\mathrm{HS}}<\infty,$$ where the second bound is for fixed $\delta,K,\Omega$.
  - Explanation: Existence of a lower triangular coefficient matrix and a symmetric path joining K to minus K that solves the regularized differential equation with uniform bounds.

        "exact_quote": "Under these assumptions, there are rows $a_i\\in H^1(\\mathbb R;\\mathbb R^m)$, $i\\in\\mathcal I$, each a linear combination of eigenfunctions with eigenvalue $-k_i^2$, such that $$-a_i''-a_iW=-k_i^2a_i\n \\quad\\text{in distributions.}\n \\label{comp:row-eigenfunction}$$ The rows $a_i$ are allowed to vanish.         {
          "anchor": "stmt-rig-fixed-right-spaces",
          "exact_quote": "For every $\\alpha$, the subspace $$\\mathcal R_\\alpha=(\\ker G_\\alpha(x))^\\perp\\subset\\mathbb R^m$$ is independent of $x\\in\\mathbb R$. The nonzero spaces $\\mathcal R_\\alpha$ are mutually orthogonal, and $$\\label{rig:dimension-bound}\n \\sum_\\alpha\\dim\\mathcal R_\\alpha\\le m.$$ In particular, only finitely many $G_\\alpha$ are nonzero. The potential $W$ has a continuous representative, equal to zero on the orthogonal complement of these spaces and equal to $q(G_\\alpha^TG_\\alpha)^r$ on $\\mathcal R_\\alpha$. With this representative, every $G_\\alpha$ is $C^2$.",
          "source_id": "PM-262.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "For every $\\alpha$, the subspace $$\\mathcal R_\\alpha=(\\ker G_\\alpha(x))^\\perp\\subset\\mathbb R^m$$ is independent of $x\\in\\mathbb R$.       "explanation": "The row spaces of the blocks are constant in position, mutually orthogonal, at most m in total dimension, and the potential has a continuous representative.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-rig-fixed-left-spaces",
        "exact_quote": "For each $\\alpha$ with $\\ell:=\\dim\\mathcal R_\\alpha>0$, the range of $G_\\alpha(x)$ in $\\mathbb R^{n_\\alpha}$ is independent of $x$. In constant orthonormal bases of this range and of $\\mathcal R_\\alpha$, the restriction of $G_\\alpha$ is an invertible $C^2$ matrix $D:\\mathbb R\\to\\mathbb R^{\\ell\\times\\ell}$. The matrix $B_0=D'D^{-1}$ is $C^1$ and satisfies, everywhere on $\\mathbb R$, $$\\label{rig:active-identities}\n B_0=B_0^T,\\qquad\n \\alpha^2I-B_0^2=(DD^T)^r>0,\\qquad\n B_0'=r(B_0^2-\\alpha^2I).$$",
        "source_id": "PM-262.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-rig-fixed-left-spaces",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-trace-deficit",
        "exact_quote": "Let $n\\geq1$ and let $B,V\\in\\mathbb C^{n\\times n}$ be Hermitian. Suppose that $$\\label{trace:symbol}\n Y_s=(sI-B)^2,\\qquad X_s=Y_s+V\\geq bI\n \\quad(s\\in\\mathbb R)$$ for some $b>0$. Set $$\\label{trace:resolvents}\n R(t)=\\frac1\\pi\\int_\\mathbb R(X_s+tI)^{-1}\\,ds,\n \\qquad P(t)=t^{-1/2}I-R(t),\\qquad t>0,$$ and $$\\label{trace:N}\n N=c_\\sigma\\int_0^\\infty t^\\beta P(t)\\,dt.$$ These integrals converge absolutely in matrix norm, and $R(t)>0$. There are continuous Hermitian matrices $L(t)=R(t)^{-1}>0$ and $Z(t)$ satisfying $$\\label{trace:LZ}\n V+tI=L^2+Z^2+i[B,Z],\\qquad\n LZ+ZL+i[B,L]=0.$$ If $N\\geq0$, then $$\\label{trace:deficit-identity}\n \\begin{split}\n \\mathop{\\mathrm{tr}}(NV)-\\mathop{\\mathrm{tr}}(N^q)\n =c_\\sigma\\int_0^\\infty t^\\beta\\Bigl(\n    \\mathop{\\mathrm{tr}}(RZ^2)\n    +q\\bigl\\|R^{-1/2}-R^{1/2}(tI+N^r)^{1/2}\\bigr\\|_{\\mathrm{HS}}^2\n    \\Bigr)\\,dt.\n \\end{split}$$ In particular, $\\mathop{\\mathrm{tr}}(NV)\\geq\\mathop{\\mathrm{tr}}(N^q)$.",
        "source_id": "PM-262.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-trace-deficit",
          "exact_quote": "Let $n\\geq1$ and let $B,V\\in\\mathbb C^{n\\times n}$ be Hermitian. Set $$\\label{trace:resolvents}\n R(t)=\\frac1\\pi\\int_\\mathbb R(X_s+tI)^{-1}\\,ds,\n \\qquad P(t)=t^{-1/2}I-R(t),\\qquad t>0,$$ and $$\\label{trace:N}\n N=c_\\sigma\\int_0^\\infty t^\\beta P(t)\\,dt.$$ These integrals converge absolutely in matrix norm, and $R(t)>0$. There are continuous Hermitian matrices $L(t)=R(t)^{-1}>0$ and $Z(t)$ satisfying $$\\label{trace:LZ}\n V+tI=L^2+Z^2+i[B,Z],\\qquad\n LZ+ZL+i[B,L]=0.$$ If $N\\geq0$, then $$\\label{trace:deficit-identity}\n \\begin{split}\n \\mathop{\\mathrm{tr}}(NV)-\\mathop{\\mathrm{tr}}(N^q)\n =c_\\sigma\\int_0^\\infty t^\\beta\\Bigl(\n    \\mathop{\\mathrm{tr}}(RZ^2)\n    +q\\bigl\\|R^{-1/2}-R^{1/2}(tI+N^r)^{1/2}\\bigr\\|_{\\mathrm{HS}}^2\n    \\Bigr)\\,dt.\n \\end{split}$$ In particular, $\\mathop{\\mathrm{tr}}(NV)\\geq\\mathop{\\mathrm{tr}}(N^q)$.",
          "source_id": "PM-262.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Let $n\\geq1$ and let $B,V\\in\\mathbb C^{n\\times n}$ be Hermitian. Set $$\\label{trace:resolvents}\n R(t)=\\frac1\\pi\\int_\\mathbb R(X_s+tI)^{-1}\\,ds,\n \\qquad P(t)=t^{-1/2}I-R(t),\\qquad t>0,$$ and $$\\label{trace:N}\n N=c_\\sigma\\int_0^\\infty t^\\beta P(t)\\,dt.$$ These integrals converge absolutely in matrix norm, and $R(t)>0$. There are continuous Hermitian matrices $L(t)=R(t)^{-1}>0$ and $Z(t)$ satisfying $$\\label{trace:LZ}\n V+tI=L^2+Z^2+i[B,Z],\\qquad\n LZ+ZL+i[B,L]=0.$$ If $N\\geq0$, then $$\\label{trace:deficit-identity}\n \\begin{split}\n \\mathop{\\mathrm{tr}}(NV)-\\mathop{\\mathrm{tr}}(N^q)\n =c_\\sigma\\int_0^\\infty t^\\beta\\Bigl(\n    \\mathop{\\mathrm{tr}}(RZ^2)\n    +q\\bigl\\|R^{-1/2}-R^{1/2}(tI+N^r)^{1/2}\\bigr\\|_{\\mathrm{HS}}^2\n    \\Bigr)\\,dt.\n \\end{split}$$ In particular, $\\mathop{\\mathrm{tr}}(NV)\\geq\\mathop{\\mathrm{tr}}(N^q)$.",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-field-properties",
        "exact_quote": "The limit in <a href=\"#field:definition\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"field:definition\">[field:definition]</a> exists locally uniformly, and $M_\\delta:\\mathop{\\mathrm{Sym}}_n\\to\\mathop{\\mathrm{Sym}}_n$ is locally Lipschitz. There is a $C^1$ function $J_\\delta:\\mathop{\\mathrm{Sym}}_n\\to\\mathbb R$, normalized by $J_\\delta(0)=0$, such that $$\\label{field:primitive}\n dJ_\\delta(B)[H]=-\\mathop{\\mathrm{tr}}(M_\\delta(B)H).$$ The scalar function $\\mu_\\delta$ is locally Lipschitz and strictly increasing, with $\\mu_\\delta(0)=0$, and $$\\label{field:mu-positive}\n       \\mu_\\delta(z)=(z+\\delta)^\\sigma-\\delta^\\sigma\n       \\qquad(z\\geq0).$$ For every real unit vector $e$, and for every standard basis vector $e_i$, respectively, $$\\begin{aligned}\n e^{\\mathsf T}M_\\delta(B)e\n  &\\leq\\mu_\\delta\\bigl(e^{\\mathsf T}K^2e\n                         -(e^{\\mathsf T}Be)^2\\bigr),\n       \\label{field:direction}\\\\\n Be_i=ye_i\\quad&\\Longrightarrow\\quad\n M_\\delta(B)e_i=\\mu_\\delta(k_i^2-y^2)e_i.\n       \\label{field:coordinate}\n\\end{aligned}$$ For each diagonal sign matrix $S$, $$\\label{field:equivariance}\n                 M_\\delta(SBS)=SM_\\delta(B)S.$$ Finally, $$\\label{field:endpoints}\n \\begin{split}\n J_\\delta(-K)-J_\\delta(K)\n &=\\sum_{i=1}^n\\int_{-k_i}^{k_i}\\mu_\\delta(k_i^2-s^2)\\,ds\\\\\n &\\longrightarrow b_\\sigma\\sum_{i=1}^n k_i^{2\\sigma+1}\n       \\qquad(\\delta\\downarrow0).\n \\end{split}$$",
        "source_id": "PM-262.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-field-properties",
          "exact_quote": "The limit in <a href=\"#field:definition\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"field:definition\">[field:definition]</a> exists locally uniformly, and $M_\\delta:\\mathop{\\mathrm{Sym}}_n\\to\\mathop{\\mathrm{Sym}}_n$ is locally Lipschitz. There is a $C^1$ function $J_\\delta:\\mathop{\\mathrm{Sym}}_n\\to\\mathbb R$, normalized by $J_\\delta(0)=0$, such that $$\\label{field:primitive}\n dJ_\\delta(B)[H]=-\\mathop{\\mathrm{tr}}(M_\\delta(B)H).$$ The scalar function $\\mu_\\delta$ is locally Lipschitz and strictly increasing, with $\\mu_\\delta(0)=0$, and $$\\label{field:mu-positive}\n       \\mu_\\delta(z)=(z+\\delta)^\\sigma-\\delta^\\sigma\n       \\qquad(z\\geq0).$$ For every real unit vector $e$, and for every standard basis vector $e_i$, respectively, $$\\begin{aligned}\n e^{\\mathsf T}M_\\delta(B)e\n  &\\leq\\mu_\\delta\\bigl(e^{\\mathsf T}K^2e\n                         -(e^{\\mathsf T}Be)^2\\bigr),\n       \\label{field:direction}\\\\\n Be_i=ye_i\\quad&\\Longrightarrow\\quad\n M_\\delta(B)e_i=\\mu_\\delta(k_i^2-y^2)e_i.\n       \\label{field:coordinate}\n\\end{aligned}$$ For each diagonal sign matrix $S$, $$\\label{field:equivariance}\n                 M_\\delta(SBS)=SM_\\delta(B)S.$$ Finally, $$\\label{field:endpoints}\n \\begin{split}\n J_\\delta(-K)-J_\\delta(K)\n &=\\sum_{i=1}^n\\int_{-k_i}^{k_i}\\mu_\\delta(k_i^2-s^2)\\,ds\\\\\n &\\longrightarrow b_\\sigma\\sum_{i=1}^n k_i^{2\\sigma+1}\n       \\qquad(\\delta\\downarrow0).\n \\end{split}$$",
          "source_id": "PM-262.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "The limit in <a href=\"#field:definition\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"field:definition\">[field:definition]</a> exists locally uniformly, and $M_\\delta:\\mathop{\\mathrm{Sym}}_n\\to\\mathop{\\mathrm{Sym}}_n$ is locally Lipschitz. There is a $C^1$ function $J_\\delta:\\mathop{\\mathrm{Sym}}_n\\to\\mathbb R$, normalized by $J_\\delta(0)=0$, such that $$\\label{field:primitive}\n dJ_\\delta(B)[H]=-\\mathop{\\mathrm{tr}}(M_\\delta(B)H).$$ The scalar function $\\mu_\\delta$ is locally Lipschitz and strictly increasing, with $\\mu_\\delta(0)=0$, and $$\\label{field:mu-positive}\n       \\mu_\\delta(z)=(z+\\delta)^\\sigma-\\delta^\\sigma\n       \\qquad(z\\geq0).$$ For every real unit vector $e$, and for every standard basis vector $e_i$, respectively, $$\\begin{aligned}\n e^{\\mathsf T}M_\\delta(B)e\n  &\\leq\\mu_\\delta\\bigl(e^{\\mathsf T}K^2e\n                         -(e^{\\mathsf T}Be)^2\\bigr),\n       \\label{field:direction}\\\\\n Be_i=ye_i\\quad&\\Longrightarrow\\quad\n M_\\delta(B)e_i=\\mu_\\delta(k_i^2-y^2)e_i.\n       \\label{field:coordinate}\n\\end{aligned}$$ For each diagonal sign matrix $S$, $$\\label{field:equivariance}\n                 M_\\delta(SBS)=SM_\\delta(B)S.$$ Finally, $$\\label{field:endpoints}\n \\begin{split}\n J_\\delta(-K)-J_\\delta(K)\n &=\\sum_{i=1}^n\\int_{-k_i}^{k_i}\\mu_\\delta(k_i^2-s^2)\\,ds\\\\\n &\\longrightarrow b_\\sigma\\sum_{i=1}^n k_i^{2\\sigma+1}\n       \\qquad(\\delta\\downarrow0).\n \\end{split}$$",
      "explanation": "The regularized matrix field exists, is locally Lipschitz, has a primitive, and satisfies directional, coordinate and equivariance relations with an explicit endpoint limit.",

There is a $C^1$ function $J_\\delta:\\mathop{\\mathrm{Sym}}_n\\to\\mathbb R$, normalized by $J_\\delta(0)=0$, such that $$\\label{field:primitive}\n dJ_\\delta(B)[H]=-\\mathop{\\mathrm{tr}}(M_\\delta(B)H).$$ The scalar function $\\mu_\\delta$ is locally Lipschitz and strictly increasing, with $\\mu_\\delta(0)=0$, and $$\\label{field:mu-positive}\n       \\mu_\\delta(z)=(z+\\delta)^\\sigma-\\delta^\\sigma\n       \\qquad(z\\geq0).$$ For every real unit vector $e$, and for every standard basis vector $e_i$, respectively, $$\\begin{aligned}\n e^{\\mathsf T}M_\\delta(B)e\n  &\\leq\\mu_\\delta\\bigl(e^{\\mathsf T}K^2e\n                         -(e^{\\mathsf T}Be)^2\\bigr),\n       \\label{field:direction}\\\\\n Be_i=ye_i\\quad&\\Longrightarrow\\quad\n M_\\delta(B)e_i=\\mu_\\delta(k_i^2-y^2)e_i.\n       \\label{field:coordinate}\n\\end{aligned}$$ For each diagonal sign matrix $S$, $$\\label{field:equivariance}\n                 M_\\delta(SBS)=SM_\\delta(B)S.$$ Finally, $$\\label{field:endpoints}\n \\begin{split}\n J_\\delta(-K)-J_\\delta(K)\n &=\\sum_{i=1}^n\\int_{-k_i}^{k_i}\\mu_\\delta(k_i^2-s^2)\\,ds\\\\\n &\\longrightarrow b_\\sigma\\sum_{i=1}^n k_i^{2\\sigma+1}\n       \\qquad(\\delta\\downarrow0).\n \\end{split}$$",

      "kind": "construction",
      "quantifiers": [

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-field-positive",
        "exact_quote": "If $M=M_\\delta(B)\\geq0$, then $$\\label{field:positive-bounds}\n Q_s(B)\\geq0\\quad(s\\in\\mathbb R),\\qquad\n             0\\leq M\\leq\\kappa^{2\\sigma}I.$$ Set $V=K^2-B^2+\\delta I$ and use $B,V$ in <a href=\"#trace:resolvents\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"trace:resolvents\">[trace:resolvents]</a> and <a href=\"#trace:N\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"trace:N\">[trace:N]</a>. Then the symbol $(sI-B)^2+V=Q_s(B)+\\delta I$ is bounded below by $\\delta I$, and $$\\label{field:N}\n                         N=M+\\delta^\\sigma I.$$ In particular, the hypotheses of Theorem <a href=\"#trace:deficit\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"trace:deficit\">10</a> hold.",
        "source_id": "PM-262.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-field-positive",
          "exact_quote": "If $M=M_\\delta(B)\\geq0$, then $$\\label{field:positive-bounds}\n Q_s(B)\\geq0\\quad(s\\in\\mathbb R),\\qquad\n             0\\leq M\\leq\\kappa^{2\\sigma}I.$$ Set $V=K^2-B^2+\\delta I$ and use $B,V$ in <a href=\"#trace:resolvents\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"trace:resolvents\">[trace:resolvents]</a> and <a href=\"#trace:N\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"trace:N\">[trace:N]</a>. Then the symbol $(sI-B)^2+V=Q_s(B)+\\delta I$ is bounded below by $\\delta I$, and $$\\label{field:N}\n                         N=M+\\delta^\\sigma I.$$ In particular, the hypotheses of Theorem <a href=\"#trace:deficit\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"trace:deficit\">10</a> hold.",
          "source_id": "PM-262.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "If $M=M_\\delta(B)\\geq0$, then $$\\label{field:positive-bounds}\n Q_s(B)\\geq0\\quad(s\\in\\mathbb R),\\qquad\n             0\\leq M\\leq\\kappa^{2\\sigma}I.$$ Set $V=K^2-B^2+\\delta I$ and use $B,V$ in <a href=\"#trace:resolvents\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"trace:resolvents\">[trace:resolvents]</a> and <a href=\"#trace:N\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"trace:N\">[trace:N]</a>. Then the symbol $(sI-B)^2+V=Q_s(B)+\\delta I$ is bounded below by $\\delta I$, and $$\\label{field:N}\n                         N=M+\\delta^\\sigma I.$$ In particular, the hypotheses of Theorem <a href=\"#trace:deficit\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"trace:deficit\">10</a> hold.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-path-connecting",
        "exact_quote": "Let $\\Omega=(x_-,x_+)$ be bounded and let $U\\in H^1_0(\\Omega;\\mathbb R^{n\\times d})$, where $n,d\\geq1$, satisfy $$\\int_\\Omega UU^{\\mathsf T}\\,dx=I_n.$$ For every $\\delta,\\varepsilon>0$ there are $C_\\varepsilon\\in\\mathcal L_n$ and $B_\\varepsilon\\in C^1(\\overline\\Omega;\\mathop{\\mathrm{Sym}}_n)$ such that, with $A_\\varepsilon=C_\\varepsilon U$, $$\\label{path:ode}\n \\varepsilon B_\\varepsilon'\n       =M_\\delta(B_\\varepsilon)-A_\\varepsilon A_\\varepsilon^{\\mathsf T},\n \\qquad B_\\varepsilon(x_-)=K,\\quad B_\\varepsilon(x_+)=-K.$$ They satisfy $$\\label{path:bounds}\n \\sup_{x\\in\\overline\\Omega}\\|B_\\varepsilon(x)\\|_{\\mathop{\\mathrm{op}}}\\leq\\kappa,\n \\qquad\n \\sup_{0<\\varepsilon\\leq1}\\|C_\\varepsilon\\|_{\\mathrm{HS}}<\\infty,$$ where the second bound is for fixed $\\delta,K,\\Omega$.",
        "source_id": "PM-262.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-path-connecting",
          "exact_quote": "Let $\\Omega=(x_-,x_+)$ be bounded and let $U\\in H^1_0(\\Omega;\\mathbb R^{n\\times d})$, where $n,d\\geq1$, satisfy $$\\int_\\Omega UU^{\\mathsf T}\\,dx=I_n.$$ For every $\\delta,\\varepsilon>0$ there are $C_\\varepsilon\\in\\mathcal L_n$ and $B_\\varepsilon\\in C^1(\\overline\\Omega;\\mathop{\\mathrm{Sym}}_n)$ such that, with $A_\\varepsilon=C_\\varepsilon U$, $$\\label{path:ode}\n \\varepsilon B_\\varepsilon'\n       =M_\\delta(B_\\varepsilon)-A_\\varepsilon A_\\varepsilon^{\\mathsf T},\n \\qquad B_\\varepsilon(x_-)=K,\\quad B_\\varepsilon(x_+)=-K.$$ They satisfy $$\\label{path:bounds}\n \\sup_{x\\in\\overline\\Omega}\\|B_\\varepsilon(x)\\|_{\\mathop{\\mathrm{op}}}\\leq\\kappa,\n \\qquad\n \\sup_{0<\\varepsilon\\leq1}\\|C_\\varepsilon\\|_{\\mathrm{HS}}<\\infty,$$ where the second bound is for fixed $\\delta,K,\\Omega$.",
          "source_id": "PM-262.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Let $\\Omega=(x_-,x_+)$ be bounded and let $U\\in H^1_0(\\Omega;\\mathbb R^{n\\times d})$, where $n,d\\geq1$, satisfy $$\\int_\\Omega UU^{\\mathsf T}\\,dx=I_n.$$ For every $\\delta,\\varepsilon>0$ there are $C_\\varepsilon\\in\\mathcal L_n$ and $B_\\varepsilon\\in C^1(\\overline\\Omega;\\mathop{\\mathrm{Sym}}_n)$ such that, with $A_\\varepsilon=C_\\varepsilon U$, $$\\label{path:ode}\n \\varepsilon B_\\varepsilon'\n       =M_\\delta(B_\\varepsilon)-A_\\varepsilon A_\\varepsilon^{\\mathsf T},\n \\qquad B_\\varepsilon(x_-)=K,\\quad B_\\varepsilon(x_+)=-K.$$ They satisfy $$\\label{path:bounds}\n \\sup_{x\\in\\overline\\Omega}\\|B_\\varepsilon(x)\\|_{\\mathop{\\mathrm{op}}}\\leq\\kappa,\n \\qquad\n \\sup_{0<\\varepsilon\\leq1}\\|C_\\varepsilon\\|_{\\mathrm{HS}}<\\infty,$$ where the second bound is for fixed $\\delta,K,\\Omega$.",

      "id": "claim-connecting-path",

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **exponent parameters** ([source](#intro:parameters)): $$\label{intro:parameters} \sigma=\gamma-\tfrac12\in(0,1),\qquad p=1+\sigma,\qquad r=\sigma^{-1},\qquad q=r+1,$$ and $$\label{intro:constants} D_\sigma=\frac{\sigma^\sigma}{(1+\sigma)^{1+\sigma}},\qquad b_\sigma=\int_{-1}^1(1-s^2)^\sigma\,ds,\qquad C_\gamma=\frac{D_\sigma}{b_\sigma}.$$ The last identity follows from the beta integral. We use the operator norm $\\|\cdot\\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}$ and the Hilbert–Schmidt norm $\\|A\\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(A^*A)$. The symbol $T$ denotes transpose of a real matrix, and $\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_n$ is the space of real symmetric $n\times n$ matrices with inner product $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(BH)$. The commutator is $[B,H]=BH-HB$. The identity matrix, or identity operator, has the size determined by its context.
- **sharp constants** ([source](#intro:constants)): $$\label{intro:constants} D_\sigma=\frac{\sigma^\sigma}{(1+\sigma)^{1+\sigma}},\qquad b_\sigma=\int_{-1}^1(1-s^2)^\sigma\,ds,\qquad C_\gamma=\frac{D_\sigma}{b_\sigma}.$$ The last identity follows from the beta integral. We use the operator norm $\\|\cdot\\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}$ and the Hilbert–Schmidt norm $\\|A\\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(A^*A)$. The symbol $T$ denotes transpose of a real matrix, and $\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_n$ is the space of real symmetric $n\times n$ matrices with inner product $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(BH)$. The commutator is $[B,H]=BH-HB$. The identity matrix, or identity operator, has the size determined by its context.
- **trace Young defect** ([source](#pre:young-defect)): $$\label{pre:young-defect} Y(W,Z)=D_\sigma\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(W^p)-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(WZ)+\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(Z^q).$$ Then $Y(W,Z)\ge0$, with equality if and only if $W=qZ^r$. Also, $$\label{pre:young-coercive} Y(W,Z)\ge\tfrac12\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(Z^q) -(2^{p-1}-1)D_\sigma\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(W^p).$$
- **action functional** ([source](#act:energy)): $$\label{act:energy} \mathcal E_K(A)=\int_\Omega \bigl(\\|A'\\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2+\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(K^2\rho)-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(\rho^q)\bigr)\,dx.$$ For pointwise matrix values $A\in\mathbb R^{n\times d}$ and $B\in\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_n$, put $$\label{act:defect} \Phi_K(A,B)=\min\left\{1, \\|[B,K^2]\\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}^2 +\\|K^2-B^2-(AA^{\mathsf T})^r\\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}^2\right\}.$$ Thus $\Phi_K=0$ expresses two algebraic relations, while $A'-BA=0$ is a first-order differential relation.
- **algebraic defect** ([source](#act:defect)): $$\label{act:defect} \Phi_K(A,B)=\min\left\{1, \\|[B,K^2]\\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}^2 +\\|K^2-B^2-(AA^{\mathsf T})^r\\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}^2\right\}.$$ Thus $\Phi_K=0$ expresses two algebraic relations, while $A'-BA=0$ is a first-order differential relation.
- **realification** ([source](#bound:realification)): For complex Hermitian $W=E+iF$, with $E,F$ real, its realification is $$\label{bound:realification} \widetilde W=\begin{pmatrix}E&-F\\F&E\end{pmatrix}.$$ The constant unitary matrix $2^{-1/2}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}I&iI\\I&-iI\end{smallmatrix}\right)$ conjugates $\widetilde W$ to $W\oplus\overline W$. Hence $\widetilde W$ is real symmetric and positive semidefinite, $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(\widetilde W^p)=2\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(W^p)$, and $\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(H_{\widetilde W})_-^\gamma=2\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(H_W)_-^\gamma$. Here conjugation identifies the spectra of $H_W$ and $H_{\overline W}$. The real inequality, divided by two, proves the result. ◻
- **normalization constant** ([source](#trace:normalization)): The matrix comparison needed in the action argument has an exact remainder. We give the proof from [[MLT](#ref-MLT), Section 3], retaining both nonnegative terms. Together they control the commutator and the algebraic equality relation used in the action argument. Throughout this appendix, $0<\sigma<1$, $\beta=\sigma-\tfrac12$, and $r=1/\sigma$, $p=1+\sigma$, $q=1+r$. Define $c_\sigma>0$ by $$\label{trace:normalization} c_\sigma^{-1} =\int_0^\infty t^\beta \bigl(t^{-1/2}-(t+1)^{-1/2}\bigr)\,dt.$$ The integrand is $O(t^{\sigma-1})$ at zero and $O(t^{\sigma-2})$ at infinity. Scaling therefore gives $$\label{trace:scaling} c_\sigma\int_0^\infty t^\beta \bigl(t^{-1/2}-(t+y)^{-1/2}\bigr)\,dt=y^\sigma, \qquad y\geq0.$$

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-spectral** [claim](#stmt-pre-spectral) / [proof](#proof-stmt-pre-spectral): Sobolev and Hölder estimates give a form bound; compactness of the truncated multiplier gives discreteness. -> closed form and discrete negative spectrum
- **step-young** [claim](#stmt-pre-young) / [proof](#proof-stmt-pre-young): Scalar maximization plus strict convexity in an eigenbasis of the density. -> nonnegative defect and equality condition
- **step-trace-deficit** [claim](#stmt-trace-deficit) / [proof](#app:trace): Half-line boundary maps, a logarithmic integral and a noncommutative square. -> exact deficit identity
- **step-field** [claim](#stmt-field-properties) / [proof](#proof-stmt-field-properties): Symmetric-cutoff integral of a functional calculus, trace differentiation for the primitive. -> locally Lipschitz field with primitive
- **step-field-positive** [claim](#stmt-field-positive) / [proof](#proof-stmt-field-positive): Directional bound forces positivity; Fubini identifies the field with the resolvent integral. -> hypotheses of the trace deficit theorem
- **step-path** [claim](#stmt-path-connecting) / [proof](#proof-stmt-path-connecting): Homotopy in a continuation parameter with sign-group equivariance, Sard's theorem and a parity argument. -> coefficient matrix and connecting path
- **step-constraint** [claim](#stmt-act-constraint) / [proof](#proof-stmt-act-constraint): Apply the trace deficit, extract small squares by averaging, then compare the deficit with the nonlinear term. -> coercivity of the algebraic defect
- **step-action** [claim](#stmt-act-quantitative-action) / [proof](#proof-stmt-act-constraint-2): Exact action identity along the connecting path, penalty limit, then removal of regularization. -> quantitative action lower bound
- **step-sharp** [claim](#stmt-bound-sharp) / [proof](#proof-stmt-bound-sharp): Trial functions, action bound, Young inequality; realification for complex potentials. -> sharp spectral inequality
- **step-relations** [claim](#stmt-comp-relations) / [proof](#comp:approximation): Finite spectral approximations with vanishing errors, fixed-row convergence, tail control through rank at most m. -> pointwise relations at equality
- **step-fixed-right** [claim](#stmt-rig-fixed-right-spaces) / [proof](#proof-stmt-rig-fixed-right-spaces): Gronwall argument makes kernels constant; continuity gives orthogonality. -> constant orthogonal row spaces
- **step-fixed-left** [claim](#stmt-rig-fixed-left-spaces) / [proof](#proof-stmt-rig-fixed-left-spaces): Differentiate the range projection to show it is constant, then derive the Riccati equation. -> invertible block with Riccati drift
- **step-main** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#pm-h-17): Diagonalize the Riccati solution, treat complex potentials by realification, verify attainment by factorization. -> equality classification

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-equality-decoupled-solitons**: For exponents in the open interval and finite matrix size, an equality case of the sharp matrix inequality is a direct sum, in one constant unitary basis, of scalar hyperbolic-secant profiles and zero channels, with independent scales and centers.
- **inv-one-bound-state-per-channel**: Each nonzero channel of an extremal potential carries exactly one negative eigenvalue, so an extremal potential has at most as many negative eigenvalues as the matrix size.
- **inv-finite-fixed-channels**: The nonzero channel row spaces are constant in position, mutually orthogonal, and only finitely many are nonzero.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-endpoint-multisolitons**: Applying the one-bound-state-per-channel conclusion at the semiclassical endpoint exponent. -> At the endpoint the equality structure differs and several bound states can sit in one scalar channel, so the classification does not describe the optimizers.
- **anti-rotating-channels**: Assuming extremal matrix potentials may have internal channels that rotate with position. -> The classification forces one constant unitary basis, so position-dependent rotation of the eigenbasis never attains equality.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `not_applicable`. The main results are analytic classification and rigidity theorems for operators on the real line, proved by variational, functional-analytic and degree-theoretic arguments (compactness, Sard-type regular values, Riccati uniqueness). No finite procedure with explicit steps computes an object from finite input, so no runnable reference implementation or exhaustive oracle can decide the statements.

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper proves a classification of equality cases for a continuous matrix Schrödinger operator. It gives no procedure with inputs and outputs that a program could execute, so a library implementing it would invent content. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No time-stepping or simulation scheme is given. The matrix ODE appears only as a uniqueness argument inside a proof, not as a numerical integrator. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The result is a characterization, not a checker. Testing whether a given potential is extremal would need an effective test for equality in a spectral inequality, and the paper provides none. |
| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The proofs are existential: limits of approximating sequences, compactness arguments and a parity argument for zeros. No finite step-by-step procedure with a complexity bound is stated, so no pseudocode is offered. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The matrix spaces and norms are analytic notation. No storage layout, index structure or operation cost is defined. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The only structural property that resembles a hardware concern is compactness of an operator between function spaces. That is an analytic statement with no bearing on arithmetic units or memory. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper contains no hardness assumption, adversary, key or protocol. It concerns binding energy of Schrödinger operators. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The matrix Young inequality with its equality condition is a convex-duality fact for positive semidefinite matrices. There is no learning problem, data or generalization claim. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Equality in a sharp inequality identifies maximizers of a spectral functional over an infinite-dimensional class. The paper proves this by analysis and offers no solver or discrete optimization model. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The channels are orthogonal internal components of a vector-valued wave function, not communicating nodes. The paper has no messages, faults or consensus. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The natural audience is mathematical physics, for example certifying that extremal multi-channel wires decouple into scalar solitons. This remains a theorem about real-line operators, with no construction to implement. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-open-exponent-interval** (hypothesis): The classification, with one bound state per nonzero channel, is asserted only for exponents strictly inside the open interval, where the strict inequality r>1 holds. The endpoint 3/2 has a different equality structure. Blocked use: ['Applying the one-bound-state-per-channel classification at the endpoint exponent 3/2', 'Importing the sech-profile description to multisoliton optimizers']
- **limit-finite-matrix-size** (hypothesis): The matrix size m is finite and fixed together with the exponent range. The number of nonzero channels is bounded by m, and the tail control relies on exactly m columns. Blocked use: ['Extending the classification to infinite-dimensional operator-valued potentials', 'Quoting a channel count larger than m']
- **limit-equality-setting** (hypothesis): The pointwise-relations argument is carried out for real symmetric nonnegative potentials with a finite, positive trace-power integral. Complex Hermitian potentials are reached only afterwards by realification. Blocked use: ['Applying the pointwise relations to sign-indefinite or non-Hermitian potentials', 'Using the argument for a potential with zero or infinite trace-power integral']
- **limit-tail-rank-bound** (edge_case): Convergence of the row matrices when the eigenvalue list is infinite depends on the rank bound coming from the fixed number of columns. It does not depend on the number of retained eigenfunctions. Blocked use: ['Reusing the tail bound when the number of columns is not fixed', 'Treating the HS-norm control as uniform over growing matrix size']
- **limit-pointwise-operator-nonconstructive** (nonconstructive): The auxiliary self-adjoint operator is obtained by subsequence compactness at each point. No measurable choice of it as a function of position is constructed. Blocked use: ['Reading the argument as an algorithm that computes the operator', 'Assuming the operator is a measurable function of position']
- **limit-path-existence-only** (nonconstructive): The connecting path and its coefficient matrix are shown to exist by a degree-parity contradiction with regular values. The proof gives no procedure that finds them. Blocked use: ['Presenting the continuation argument as a root-finding algorithm with guaranteed convergence', 'Claiming an explicit formula or computable bound for the zero']
- **limit-action-constant-dependence** (formal_scope): The defect coefficient is uniform in the number of rows, the dimension, the interval and the trial functions. It still depends on the exponent parameter and the bound on the energies. Blocked use: ['Taking the coefficient as an absolute constant independent of the exponent', 'Using it when the largest energy bound is not controlled']
- **limit-complex-by-realification** (approximation): The complex Hermitian case is deduced from the real case by doubling the matrix size, so every channel statement for complex potentials is inherited through the real classification. Blocked use: ['Treating the complex case as proved by an independent argument', 'Dropping the constant unitary change of basis between the doubled and original potentials']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We classify all equality cases in the sharp one-dimensional Lieb–Thirring inequality for every finite matrix size and $`1/2\lt \gamma\lt 3/2`$. For measurable Hermitian positive semidefinite potentials <i>W</i> with $`\int_\mathbb R\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits (W^{\gamma+1/2})\lt \infty`$, equality holds precisely for direct sums, in one constant unitary basis, of scalar one-bound-state solitons and zero channels. The nonzero solitons may have independent scales and centers.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>

<a id="intro:section"></a>
### Introduction

Lieb–Thirring inequalities compare the binding energy of a Schrödinger operator with an integral of its potential. Equality asks how the potential must arrange that binding. In one dimension, a scalar one-bound-state optimizer has a hyperbolic-secant profile. For a matrix potential, several such profiles can occupy orthogonal internal channels. The issue is whether equality also permits channels that rotate with position, or several bound states that interact within the same channel. We prove that throughout $1/2<\gamma<3/2$ every equality case consists of independent scalar profiles in one fixed basis.

<a id="intro:class"></a>
Let $m\ge1$ be finite, let $1/2<\gamma<3/2$, and set $p=\gamma+1/2$. For a measurable Hermitian positive semidefinite function $W:\mathbb R\to\mathbb C^{m\times m}$ satisfying $$\label{intro:class}
 \int_\mathbb R\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(W(x)^p)\,dx<\infty,$$ let $H_W=-d^2/dx^2-W$ be the operator associated with <a id="intro:form"></a>$$\label{intro:form}
 h_W[\psi]=\int_\mathbb R\|\psi'\|^2
       -\int_\mathbb R\langle\psi,W\psi\rangle,
 \qquad \psi\in H^1(\mathbb R;\mathbb C^m).$$ Lemma <a href="#pre:spectral" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:spectral">2</a> proves that the form is closed and bounded below and that the negative spectrum is discrete, with possible accumulation only at zero. We write $$\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(H_W)_-^\gamma=\sum_{\lambda_j(H_W)<0}|\lambda_j(H_W)|^\gamma,$$ counting multiplicities. The finite-dimensional trace $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}$ is unnormalized; matrix powers are defined by spectral functional calculus.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

<a id="intro:sharp"></a>
<a id="intro:classification"></a>
**Theorem 1** (Equality classification). *For every $W$ satisfying <a href="#intro:class" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="intro:class">[intro:class]</a>, $$\label{intro:sharp}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(H_W)_-^\gamma\le C_\gamma\int_\mathbb R\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(W^p),
 \qquad
 C_\gamma=\left(\frac{\gamma-1/2}{\gamma+1/2}\right)^{\gamma-1/2}
       \frac{\Gamma(\gamma+1)}{\sqrt\pi\,\Gamma(\gamma+3/2)}.$$ Put $r=(\gamma-1/2)^{-1}$. Equality holds if and only if there are $k\in\{0,\ldots,m\}$, a constant unitary matrix $U$, positive numbers $a_1,\ldots,a_k$, and real numbers $x_1,\ldots,x_k$ such that almost everywhere $$\label{intro:classification}
 W(x)=U\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}\bigl(w_1(x),\ldots,w_k(x),0,\ldots,0\bigr)U^*,
 \quad
 w_j(x)=(r+1)a_j^2\mathop{\mathrm{sech}}^2\!\bigl(ra_j(x-x_j)\bigr).$$ The case $k=0$ means $W=0$. Each nonzero channel has exactly one negative eigenvalue, $-a_j^2$, and therefore every extremal potential has at most $m$ negative eigenvalues, counted with multiplicity.*

</div>

The inequality in <a href="#intro:sharp" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="intro:sharp">[intro:sharp]</a> is the sharp matrix inequality of <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[MLT](#ref-MLT), Theorem 1.1]. Our contribution is its equality classification. We retain the analytic constructions in the proof, so that the inequality and its equality conditions are established here under the same measurable potential hypothesis. The scales and centers in <a href="#intro:classification" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="intro:classification">[intro:classification]</a> are independent; repeated scales and identical profiles are allowed.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### History and related results

Lieb and Thirring introduced spectral moment inequalities in their work on the kinetic energy of fermions and stability of matter <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[LiebThirring1975](#ref-LiebThirring1975), [LiebThirring1976](#ref-LiebThirring1976)]. Their sharp constants distinguish the regimes in which binding is best concentrated in a single state or distributed among many states. Keller’s optimization of the lowest Schrödinger eigenvalue <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Keller1961](#ref-Keller1961)] supplies the one-state problem underlying the scalar profiles in <a href="#intro:classification" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="intro:classification">[intro:classification]</a>; their explicit form appears in <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[LiebThirring1976](#ref-LiebThirring1976), Section 4(B), Equations (4.19)–(4.20)]. The present question concerns equality in the sum of all negative eigenvalue moments, with a matrix potential that need not commute with itself at different points.

The sharp scalar endpoint $\gamma=1/2$ was proved by Hundertmark, Lieb and Thomas <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[HLT1998](#ref-HLT1998)]; Hundertmark, Laptev and Weidl <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[HLW2000](#ref-HLW2000), Theorem 3.1] extended it to operator-valued potentials. The semiclassical constant is sharp for $\gamma\ge3/2$; the lifting of moments of Aizenman and Lieb <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[AizenmanLieb1978](#ref-AizenmanLieb1978)] and the operator-valued inequalities of Laptev and Weidl <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[LaptevWeidl2000](#ref-LaptevWeidl2000)] are central to this development. Benguria and Loss <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[BenguriaLoss2000](#ref-BenguriaLoss2000)] gave a commutation proof of the matrix result at $\gamma=3/2$. More recently, Read and Schulz <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[ReadSchulz2026](#ref-ReadSchulz2026)] proved the sharp scalar inequality at $\gamma=1$, with constant $4/(3\sqrt3\pi)$, and its operator-valued extension. The matrix companion <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[MLT](#ref-MLT)] proves the sharp one-state constant for the whole open interval considered here.

The endpoint $\gamma=3/2$ has a different equality structure. In the scalar finite-rank problem, Frank, Gontier and Lewin <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[FrankGontierLewin2025](#ref-FrankGontierLewin2025), Theorem 5] identify the optimizers as KdV multisolitons. In particular, several bound states can occur in a single scalar channel at that endpoint. The conclusion of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, one bound state per nonzero channel, uses the strict inequality $r>1$ and is asserted only for the open interval.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Quantitative defects and fixed channels

The proof begins with the finite-interval matrix action method of <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[MLT](#ref-MLT)]. For orthonormal real trial functions approximating finitely many negative eigenfunctions, place the functions in the rows of $U(x)$ and choose a lower triangular matrix $C$. The rows of $A=CU$ remain in the successive trial spans. If $K$ is the diagonal matrix of positive square roots of the trial binding energies, the action involves $A'$, $K^2AA^T$, and a power of the density $AA^T$. A symmetric matrix path $B(x)$ connects $K$ to $-K$ and gives a lower bound for that action.

For equality, the lower bound must retain more information. Besides the square $\|A'-BA\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2$, we retain a term controlling $$[B,K^2]\quad\hbox{and}\quad K^2-B^2-(AA^T)^r.$$ Its coefficient depends on the largest binding energy and the exponent, but not on the number of trial functions. This uniformity is the first essential point: an extremal potential is not known in advance to have finitely many negative eigenvalues. The integrated trace identity of the matrix companion supplies the two nonnegative squares from which this estimate follows.

The second point is compactness with a fixed number of columns. As the trial list grows, $A$ has more rows but still only $m$ columns. Its density therefore has rank at most $m$. The retained defect bounds the tail rows by the small binding energies in that tail. This yields strong pointwise convergence even for an infinite eigenvalue list and gives exact algebraic relations in the limit. Those relations separate the rows with different energies. A differential inequality then makes their row spaces independent of position; their mutual orthogonality leaves at most $m$ nonzero directions. On each resulting finite block, a matrix Riccati equation has a fixed diagonalizing basis and determines the profiles in <a href="#intro:classification" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="intro:classification">[intro:classification]</a>.

Section <a href="#pre:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:section">2</a> gives the spectral and convexity preliminaries. Section <a href="#act:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="act:section">3</a> proves the quantitative action estimate, and Section <a href="#bound:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bound:section">4</a> recovers the sharp spectral bound. Section <a href="#comp:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="comp:section">5</a> passes to exact relations for an equality case. Section <a href="#rig:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rig:section">6</a> proves that the channels are fixed, solves the Riccati equation, and treats complex potentials and the converse. Appendices <a href="#app:trace" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:trace">7</a> and <a href="#app:paths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:paths">8</a> prove the trace identity, regularized field construction, and connecting-path theorem used by the action estimate.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Notation

Throughout the proof, <a id="intro:parameters"></a>$$\label{intro:parameters}
 \sigma=\gamma-\tfrac12\in(0,1),\qquad
 p=1+\sigma,\qquad r=\sigma^{-1},\qquad q=r+1,$$ and <a id="intro:constants"></a>$$\label{intro:constants}
 D_\sigma=\frac{\sigma^\sigma}{(1+\sigma)^{1+\sigma}},\qquad
 b_\sigma=\int_{-1}^1(1-s^2)^\sigma\,ds,\qquad
 C_\gamma=\frac{D_\sigma}{b_\sigma}.$$ The last identity follows from the beta integral. We use the operator norm $\|\cdot\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}$ and the Hilbert–Schmidt norm $\|A\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(A^*A)$. The symbol $T$ denotes transpose of a real matrix, and $\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_n$ is the space of real symmetric $n\times n$ matrices with inner product $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(BH)$. The commutator is $[B,H]=BH-HB$. The identity matrix, or identity operator, has the size determined by its context.

<a id="pre:section"></a>
### Spectral and convexity preliminaries

Two elementary facts let us work with measurable potentials and identify the equality condition in the potential estimate. The first is the form argument used in the matrix inequality <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[MLT](#ref-MLT), Lemma 7.1]; we give its proof to fix the precise regularity available below.

<a id="stmt-pre-spectral"></a>
<div id="pre:spectral" class="lemma">

**Lemma 2**. *Let $p>1$ and let $W:\mathbb R\to\mathbb C^{m\times m}$ be measurable, Hermitian and positive semidefinite, with $\int_\mathbb R\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(W^p)<\infty$. The form $h_W$ on $H^1(\mathbb R;\mathbb C^m)$ is closed and bounded below. Multiplication by $W^{1/2}$ is compact from $H^1$ to $L^2$. The negative spectrum of $H_W$ consists of isolated eigenvalues of finite multiplicity, with possible accumulation only at zero. Its eigenfunctions belong to $H^1$ and satisfy $-\psi''-W\psi=\lambda\psi$ distributionally.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pre-spectral"><summary>Proof of stmt-pre-spectral</summary>

<a id="pre:form-bound"></a>
*Proof.* Set $w=\|W\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}$. Then $w^p\le\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(W^p)$, so $w\in L^p$. The one-dimensional Sobolev estimate, applied to the norm of a vector function, gives $\|\psi\|_\infty^2\le2\|\psi\|_2\|\psi'\|_2$. Hölder’s inequality, interpolation and Young’s inequality imply $$\label{pre:form-bound}
 \int_\mathbb R\langle\psi,W\psi\rangle
 \le 2^{1/p}\|w\|_p\|\psi'\|_2^{1/p}\|\psi\|_2^{2-1/p}
 \le \varepsilon\|\psi'\|_2^2+C_\varepsilon\|\psi\|_2^2.$$ A sufficiently shifted form norm is therefore equivalent to the $H^1$ norm. This proves closedness and semiboundedness, and polarization gives continuity of the potential form on $H^1$.

Write $T\psi=W^{1/2}\psi$. The truncated multiplier $T_{R,L}=\mathbf1_{\{|x|\le R,\,w\le L\}}T$ is compact by the compact embedding of $H^1([-R,R])$ into $L^2([-R,R])$. Moreover, $$\|(T-T_{R,L})\psi\|_2^2
 \le C\|w\mathbf1_{\{|x|>R\}\cup\{w>L\}}\|_p\|\psi\|_{H^1}^2.$$ The coefficient tends to zero, so $T$ is compact. For each $a>0$, an infinite-dimensional spectral subspace in $(-\infty,-a]$ would contain an $L^2$-orthonormal sequence bounded in $H^1$ by <a href="#pre:form-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pre:form-bound">[pre:form-bound]</a>. A subsequence converges weakly to zero in $H^1$, hence its images under $T$ converge strongly to zero. This contradicts $-a\ge h_W[\psi]\ge-\|T\psi\|_2^2$ for unit vectors in that subspace. Thus every such subspace is finite-dimensional. Finally, the weak form equation tested against smooth compactly supported functions is the stated distributional equation; its potential term is locally integrable because $W\in L^p_{\mathrm{loc}}$ and $\psi$ is locally bounded. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-pre-young"></a>
<div id="pre:young" class="lemma">

<a id="pre:young-coercive"></a>
**Lemma 3** (Trace Young inequality and its equality condition). *For positive semidefinite matrices $W,Z$ of the same finite size, set <a id="pre:young-defect"></a>$$\label{pre:young-defect}
 Y(W,Z)=D_\sigma\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(W^p)-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(WZ)+\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(Z^q).$$ Then $Y(W,Z)\ge0$, with equality if and only if $W=qZ^r$. Also, $$\label{pre:young-coercive}
 Y(W,Z)\ge\tfrac12\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(Z^q)
       -(2^{p-1}-1)D_\sigma\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(W^p).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pre-young"><summary>Proof of stmt-pre-young</summary>

*Proof.* Scalar maximization over $z\ge0$ gives $yz-z^q\le D_\sigma y^p$ for $y\ge0$, with equality precisely when $y=qz^r$. In an orthonormal eigenbasis $(v_j)$ of $Z$, let $z_j$ be its eigenvalues and put $y_j=\langle v_j,Wv_j\rangle$. Strict convexity of $t^p$ on $[0,\infty)$ gives $$\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(WZ)-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(Z^q)
 \le D_\sigma\sum_j y_j^p
 \le D_\sigma\sum_j\langle v_j,W^pv_j\rangle.$$ Equality in the second inequality holds exactly when each $v_j$ belongs to an eigenspace of $W$. Equality in the first then requires $Wv_j=qz_j^rv_j$ for every $j$. This proves both directions of the equality assertion, including zero eigenvalues. Applying the same inequality to $2W,Z$ gives $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(WZ)\le\tfrac12\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(Z^q)+2^{p-1}D_\sigma\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(W^p)$, which is <a href="#pre:young-coercive" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pre:young-coercive">[pre:young-coercive]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="act:section"></a>
### An action estimate that retains the equality defects

The finite-interval argument for the sharp inequality can also measure failure of its equality relations. We retain the first-order square and a quantitative part of the matrix trace deficit. The coefficient of this second defect will be independent of the number of rows. This uniformity allows us to exhaust an eigenvalue list before knowing that it is finite.

The construction adapts the field, connecting paths, and action identity of <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[MLT](#ref-MLT), Sections 3–6]. Their analytic foundations are proved in Appendices <a href="#app:trace" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:trace">7</a> and <a href="#app:paths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:paths">8</a>; the additional estimate below extracts operator-norm rigidity from the trace deficit.

Fix positive integers $n,d$, a bounded interval $\Omega=(x_-,x_+)$, and $K=\operatorname{diag}(\ell_1,\ldots,\ell_n)$ with $0<\ell_i\leq\kappa$. For $A\in H^1_0(\Omega;\mathbb R^{n\times d})$, set $\rho=AA^{\mathsf T}$ and define <a id="act:energy"></a>$$\label{act:energy}
 \mathcal E_K(A)=\int_\Omega
 \bigl(\|A'\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2+\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(K^2\rho)-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(\rho^q)\bigr)\,dx.$$ For pointwise matrix values $A\in\mathbb R^{n\times d}$ and $B\in\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_n$, put <a id="act:defect"></a>$$\label{act:defect}
 \Phi_K(A,B)=\min\left\{1,
       \|[B,K^2]\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}^2
       +\|K^2-B^2-(AA^{\mathsf T})^r\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}^2\right\}.$$ Thus $\Phi_K=0$ expresses two algebraic relations, while $A'-BA=0$ is a first-order differential relation.

<a id="stmt-act-quantitative-action"></a>
<div id="act:quantitative-action" class="theorem">

<a id="act:bound"></a>
**Theorem 4** (Quantitative finite-interval action estimate). *Let $0<\sigma<1$ and $\kappa>0$. There is a constant $c_*=c_*(\sigma,\kappa)>0$ with the following property. For $n,d,\Omega,K$ as above, suppose $U\in H^1_0(\Omega;\mathbb R^{n\times d})$ satisfies $$\int_\Omega UU^{\mathsf T}\,dx=I_n.$$ For every $\eta>0$ there exist a real lower triangular matrix $C\in\mathbb R^{n\times n}$ and a path $B\in C^1(\overline\Omega;\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_n)$ such that $$B(x_-)=K,\qquad B(x_+)=-K,\qquad
 \sup_{x\in\overline\Omega}\|B(x)\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}\leq\kappa,$$ and, for $A=CU$, $$\label{act:bound}
 \mathcal E_K(A)\geq b_\sigma\sum_{i=1}^n\ell_i^{2\gamma}
   +\int_\Omega\left(\|A'-BA\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
                        +c_*\Phi_K(A,B)\right)\,dx-\eta.$$ In particular, $c_*$ is independent of $n,d,\Omega,U$, and the smallest diagonal entry of $K$.*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Coercivity at the matrix-field constraint

<a id="act:primitive"></a>
<a id="act:endpoints"></a>
The algebraic relations $\Phi_K(A,B)=0$ require $[B,K^2]=0$ and $\rho=(K^2-B^2)^\sigma$. For $0<\delta\leq1$, we use a locally Lipschitz field $M_\delta:\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_n\to\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_n$ extending the commuting rule $$M_\delta(B)=(K^2-B^2+\delta I)^\sigma-\delta^\sigma I
 \quad\text{if }[B,K^2]=0\text{ and }K^2-B^2\geq0.$$ Thus the constraint $M_\delta(B)=\rho$ is a regularized form of the algebraic equality relation in this case. The extension to arbitrary symmetric $B$ has a $C^1$ primitive $J_\delta:\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_n\to\mathbb R$ satisfying $$\label{act:primitive}
 dJ_\delta(B)[H]=-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(M_\delta(B)H).$$ Proposition <a href="#field:properties" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="field:properties">11</a> constructs these maps and proves their required properties. Along a path $B$, the derivative identity converts $-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(M_\delta(B)B')$ into the total derivative of $J_\delta(B)$, which will supply the boundary term in the action identity. Its endpoint difference is explicit: $$\begin{aligned}
 J_\delta(-K)-J_\delta(K)
 &=\sum_{i=1}^n\int_{-\ell_i}^{\ell_i}
     \bigl((\ell_i^2-s^2+\delta)^\sigma-\delta^\sigma\bigr)\,ds
       \notag\\
 &\longrightarrow b_\sigma\sum_{i=1}^n\ell_i^{2\gamma}
       \qquad(\delta\downarrow0).
       \label{act:endpoints}
\end{aligned}$$ The limit follows by dominated convergence and $2\sigma+1=2\gamma$.

Define the scalar function <a id="act:F"></a>$$\label{act:F}
 F(A,B)=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(\rho^q)-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl((K^2-B^2)\rho\bigr),
 \qquad \rho=AA^{\mathsf T}.$$ The following lemma bounds this nonlinear term while keeping the algebraic defect. The constraint in its hypothesis will later be enforced by a penalty.

<a id="stmt-act-constraint"></a>
<div id="act:constraint" class="lemma">

<a id="act:constraint-bound"></a>
**Lemma 5**. *The constant $c_*(\sigma,\kappa)>0$ can be chosen so that, whenever $0<\delta\leq1$, $\|B\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}\leq\kappa$, and $M_\delta(B)=AA^{\mathsf T}$, one has $$\label{act:constraint-bound}
 F(A,B)+c_*\Phi_K(A,B)\leq E_{n,\delta},
 \qquad
 E_{n,\delta}
 =n\bigl(\delta\kappa^{2\sigma}
           +\delta^\sigma(\kappa^2+\delta)\bigr)
      +\delta^{2\sigma}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-act-constraint"><summary>Proof of stmt-act-constraint</summary>

<a id="act:LZ"></a>
<a id="act:trace-deficit"></a>
*Proof.* Write $\rho=M_\delta(B)\geq0$. Proposition <a href="#field:positive" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="field:positive">12</a> gives <a id="act:positive-symbol"></a>$$\label{act:positive-symbol}
 Q_s:=s^2I-2sB+K^2\geq0\quad(s\in\mathbb R),
 \qquad 0\leq\rho\leq\kappa^{2\sigma}I.$$ Put $V=K^2-B^2+\delta I$ and $\beta=\sigma-\tfrac12$. With the positive normalization constant $c_\sigma$ specified in <a href="#trace:normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="trace:normalization">[trace:normalization]</a>, define <a id="act:RN"></a>$$\label{act:RN}
 \begin{split}
 R(t)&=\frac1\pi\int_\mathbb R
           \bigl(Q_s+(\delta+t)I\bigr)^{-1}\,ds,\qquad t>0,\\
 N&=c_\sigma\int_0^\infty
           t^\beta\bigl(t^{-1/2}I-R(t)\bigr)\,dt
       =\rho+\delta^\sigma I.
 \end{split}$$ The last identity is also part of Proposition <a href="#field:positive" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="field:positive">12</a>. Since $(sI-B)^2+V=Q_s+\delta I\geq\delta I$ and $N\geq0$, Theorem <a href="#trace:deficit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="trace:deficit">10</a> applies. It supplies $L(t)=R(t)^{-1}>0$ and Hermitian matrices $Z(t)$ satisfying $$\label{act:LZ}
 V+tI=L^2+Z^2+i[B,Z],\qquad
 LZ+ZL+i[B,L]=0,$$ and the exact identity $$\begin{aligned}
 \Delta&:=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(NV)-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(N^q)\notag\\
 &=c_\sigma\int_0^\infty t^\beta
    \left(\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(R(t)Z(t)^2)
      +q\bigl\|R(t)^{-1/2}
           -R(t)^{1/2}(tI+N^r)^{1/2}\bigr\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\right)\,dt
       \geq0.
       \label{act:trace-deficit}
\end{aligned}$$ The matrices in <a href="#act:LZ" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="act:LZ">[act:LZ]</a> may be complex Hermitian even though $A$ and $B$ are real; $i$ denotes the imaginary unit.

We extract a dimension-independent bound from the part of the integral with $1\leq t\leq2$. On this interval there are constants $a_\kappa,A_\kappa>0$ such that <a id="act:R-bounds"></a>$$\label{act:R-bounds}
                       a_\kappa I\leq R(t)\leq A_\kappa I.$$ For the upper bound, use $Q_s\geq0$ on a bounded interval in $s$, and $Q_s\geq\tfrac12s^2I$ for $|s|\geq\max\{1,4\kappa\}$. For the lower bound, restrict the integral to $|s|\leq1$, where $Q_s+(\delta+t)I\leq(4+2\kappa+\kappa^2)I$. These estimates depend on $\kappa$ alone. Moreover $\|N\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}\leq\kappa^{2\sigma}+1$, so $H_t=(tI+N^r)^{1/2}$ and $L(t)$ are bounded in operator norm by constants depending only on $\sigma,\kappa$.

<a id="act:small-squares"></a>
The two integrands in <a href="#act:trace-deficit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="act:trace-deficit">[act:trace-deficit]</a> control $\|Z(t)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2$ and $\|L(t)-H_t\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2$: indeed $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(RZ^2)=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(ZRZ)$ and $$R^{-1/2}-R^{1/2}H_t=R^{1/2}(L-H_t).$$ It follows by averaging over $[1,2]$ that at some $t$ in this interval, $$\label{act:small-squares}
 \|Z(t)\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}+\|L(t)-H_t\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}
        \leq C_1\sqrt\Delta,$$ where $C_1$ depends only on $\sigma,\kappa$. When $\Delta=0$, the same conclusion follows by taking a point where both nonnegative integrands vanish.

<a id="act:V-coercivity"></a>
Suppose first that $\Delta\leq1$, and use this value of $t$. The first identity in <a href="#act:LZ" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="act:LZ">[act:LZ]</a> gives $$V-N^r=(L^2-H_t^2)+Z^2+i[B,Z].$$ The boundedness of $L,H_t,B$, together with <a href="#act:small-squares" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="act:small-squares">[act:small-squares]</a>, bounds the norm of this expression by $C_2\sqrt\Delta$. To control the commutator, the second identity in <a href="#act:LZ" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="act:LZ">[act:LZ]</a> first yields $\|[B,L]\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}\leq2\|L\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}\|Z\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}$. Then $$[B,V]=[B,L^2]+[B,Z^2]+i[B,[B,Z]]$$ has the same bound. Thus $$\label{act:V-coercivity}
 \|V-N^r\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}+\|[B,V]\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}
       \leq C_3\sqrt\Delta.$$ Here and below the constants in this proof depend only on $\sigma,\kappa$.

<a id="act:coercivity"></a>
The matrices $N=\rho+\delta^\sigma I$ and $\rho$ commute. Because $r>1$, the scalar function $y\mapsto y^r$ is Lipschitz on $[0,\kappa^{2\sigma}+1]$. Consequently $$\|N^r-\rho^r\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}\leq C_4\delta^\sigma.$$ Since $[B,V]=[B,K^2]$ and $\delta\leq\delta^\sigma$, <a href="#act:V-coercivity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="act:V-coercivity">[act:V-coercivity]</a> implies $$\|[B,K^2]\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}^2
   +\|K^2-B^2-\rho^r\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}^2
       \leq C_5(\Delta+\delta^{2\sigma}).$$ Choose $c_*>0$ sufficiently small, also with $c_*\leq1$. For $\Delta\leq1$ the preceding estimate gives $$\label{act:coercivity}
                   c_*\Phi_K(A,B)\leq\Delta+\delta^{2\sigma}.$$ For $\Delta>1$ this follows directly from $\Phi_K\leq1$. This proves the required uniform control of the algebraic defect.

It remains to relate $\Delta$ to $F$. Expanding $N$ and $V$ gives $$F(A,B)=-\Delta+\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(\rho^q)-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(N^q)
                  +\delta\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\rho+\delta^\sigma\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}V.$$ The first two powers are ordered because $N=\rho+\delta^\sigma I$. Also $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\rho\leq n\kappa^{2\sigma}$ and $V\leq(\kappa^2+\delta)I$. Adding <a href="#act:coercivity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="act:coercivity">[act:coercivity]</a> now proves <a href="#act:constraint-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="act:constraint-bound">[act:constraint-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### The action identity and the penalty limit

<details id="proof-stmt-act-constraint-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-act-constraint</summary>

<a id="act:path"></a>
*Proof of Theorem <a href="#act:quantitative-action" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="act:quantitative-action">4</a>.* Fix $0<\delta\leq1$. Proposition <a href="#path:connecting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="path:connecting">13</a> supplies, for every $0<\varepsilon\leq1$, a lower triangular matrix $C_\varepsilon$ and a path $B_\varepsilon$ with the prescribed endpoints and operator-norm bound such that, for $A_\varepsilon=C_\varepsilon U$, $$\label{act:path}
 \varepsilon B_\varepsilon'
       =M_\delta(B_\varepsilon)
                    -A_\varepsilon A_\varepsilon^{\mathsf T}.$$ For fixed $\delta,K,\Omega,U$, the matrices $C_\varepsilon$ are bounded uniformly in $0<\varepsilon\leq1$.

<a id="act:identity"></a>
Temporarily suppress the subscript $\varepsilon$ and write $$u_\delta(A,B)
                      =\|M_\delta(B)-AA^{\mathsf T}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2.$$ Since $A\in H^1_0$ and $B\in C^1$, the product and chain rules for absolutely continuous functions, <a href="#act:primitive" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="act:primitive">[act:primitive]</a>, and <a href="#act:path" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="act:path">[act:path]</a> yield almost everywhere $$\begin{aligned}
 \bigl(J_\delta(B)+\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(A^{\mathsf T}BA)\bigr)'
 &=2\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}((A')^{\mathsf T}BA)
          -\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl((M_\delta(B)-AA^{\mathsf T})B'\bigr)\\
 &=2\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}((A')^{\mathsf T}BA)-\varepsilon^{-1}u_\delta(A,B).
\end{aligned}$$ Complete the square in the action and integrate this identity. Because $A$ vanishes at both endpoints, the result is the exact formula $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal E_K(A)
 &=J_\delta(-K)-J_\delta(K)\notag\\
 &\quad+\int_\Omega\left(
       \|A'-BA\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2-F(A,B)
                          +\varepsilon^{-1}u_\delta(A,B)\right)\,dx.
       \label{act:identity}
\end{aligned}$$

<a id="act:penalty"></a>
We now use the penalty to pass from the constraint estimate in Lemma <a href="#act:constraint" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="act:constraint">5</a> to all matrix values on these paths. The continuous representative of $U$ is bounded on $\overline\Omega$. The uniform bound on $C_\varepsilon$ and the bound $\|B_\varepsilon\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}\leq\kappa$ therefore place all pairs $(A_\varepsilon(x),B_\varepsilon(x))$ in one compact set $\mathcal K_\delta\subset\mathbb R^{n\times d}\times\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_n$ on which $\|B\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}\leq\kappa$. On this set $F$, $\Phi_K$, and $u_\delta$ are continuous. Lemma <a href="#act:constraint" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="act:constraint">5</a> implies $$\label{act:penalty}
 \limsup_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}
 \sup_{(A,B)\in\mathcal K_\delta}
    \bigl(F(A,B)+c_*\Phi_K(A,B)-\varepsilon^{-1}u_\delta(A,B)\bigr)
       \leq E_{n,\delta}.$$ Indeed, if the left side exceeded $E_{n,\delta}$, some sequence $\varepsilon_j\downarrow0$ and corresponding pairs in the compact set would make the expression at least $E_{n,\delta}+\tau$ for some $\tau>0$. Boundedness of $F+c_*\Phi_K$ would force $u_\delta=O(\varepsilon_j)$ along that sequence. A convergent subsequence would then give a limiting pair with $u_\delta=0$ and $F+c_*\Phi_K\geq E_{n,\delta}+\tau$, contradicting Lemma <a href="#act:constraint" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="act:constraint">5</a>.

For any $\tau>0$, choose $\varepsilon$ small enough that the supremum in <a href="#act:penalty" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="act:penalty">[act:penalty]</a> is at most $E_{n,\delta}+\tau$. Substitution into <a href="#act:identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="act:identity">[act:identity]</a> gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal E_K(A_\varepsilon)
 \geq{}&J_\delta(-K)-J_\delta(K)
        -|\Omega|(E_{n,\delta}+\tau)\\
 &+\int_\Omega\left(\|A_\varepsilon'-B_\varepsilon A_\varepsilon\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
                +c_*\Phi_K(A_\varepsilon,B_\varepsilon)\right)\,dx.
\end{aligned}$$ Finally, first choose $\delta>0$ so small that the endpoint error in <a href="#act:endpoints" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="act:endpoints">[act:endpoints]</a> and $|\Omega|E_{n,\delta}$ together are less than $\eta/2$. Next choose $\tau>0$ with $|\Omega|\tau<\eta/2$, and then choose the required $\varepsilon$. These choices prove <a href="#act:bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="act:bound">[act:bound]</a>. All compactness arguments were made after fixing $\delta$; no bound uniform in $\delta$ is needed. ◻

</details>

<a id="bound:section"></a>
### Recovering the sharp spectral bound

The action estimate implies the sharp inequality by the spectral passage of <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[MLT](#ref-MLT), Section 7]. We record it before studying equality, so that the finite spectral moment used below has already been established.

<a id="stmt-bound-sharp"></a>
<div id="bound:sharp" class="proposition">

**Proposition 6**. *Every potential in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> satisfies $$\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(H_W)_-^\gamma\le\frac{D_\sigma}{b_\sigma}
                         \int_\mathbb R\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(W^p).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-bound-sharp"><summary>Proof of stmt-bound-sharp</summary>

<a id="bound:trial"></a>
*Proof.* First suppose that $W$ is real symmetric. Choose any finite initial list of negative eigenvalues $-k_1^2,\ldots,-k_n^2$, with $k_1\ge\cdots\ge k_n>0$, and real orthonormal eigenfunctions $\varphi_i$. For $0<\xi<k_n^2$ put $\ell_i=(k_i^2-\xi)^{1/2}$ and $K=\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(\ell_1,\ldots,\ell_n)$. There are real smooth compactly supported orthonormal functions $u_i$ with form matrix $$\label{bound:trial}
 (h_W[u_i,u_j])_{i,j=1}^n\le-K^2.$$ Indeed, approximate the $\varphi_i$ in $H^1$, then multiply the family by the inverse square root of its Gram matrix. The Gram matrix tends to $I_n$, and the form matrix tends to $-\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(k_i^2)$ by Lemma <a href="#pre:spectral" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:spectral">2</a>; an operator norm error below $\xi$ gives <a href="#bound:trial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="bound:trial">[bound:trial]</a>.

Let $U$ have rows $u_i$, and choose a bounded interval containing their supports. For every lower triangular $C$, the $i$th row $a_i$ of $A=CU$ satisfies $$h_W[a_i]+\ell_i^2\|a_i\|_2^2
 \le\sum_{l\le i}C_{il}^2(\ell_i^2-\ell_l^2)\le0.$$ Set $Z=A^TA$. The matrices $AA^T$ and $Z$ have the same nonzero eigenvalues, with multiplicity. Summing the preceding bound and applying Lemma <a href="#pre:young" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:young">3</a> yields $$\mathcal E_K(A)
 \le\int_\mathbb R\{\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(WZ)-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(Z^q)\}
 \le D_\sigma\int_\mathbb R\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(W^p).$$ Theorem <a href="#act:quantitative-action" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="act:quantitative-action">4</a>, with its nonnegative defects discarded, gives $b_\sigma\sum_i\ell_i^{2\gamma}\le D_\sigma\int\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(W^p)+\eta$ for every $\eta>0$. Let $\eta\downarrow0$, then $\xi\downarrow0$, and finally exhaust the negative eigenvalues. The case of an empty negative spectrum is immediate.

<a id="bound:realification"></a>
For complex Hermitian $W=E+iF$, with $E,F$ real, its realification is $$\label{bound:realification}
 \widetilde W=\begin{pmatrix}E&-F\\F&E\end{pmatrix}.$$ The constant unitary matrix $2^{-1/2}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}I&iI\\I&-iI\end{smallmatrix}\right)$ conjugates $\widetilde W$ to $W\oplus\overline W$. Hence $\widetilde W$ is real symmetric and positive semidefinite, $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(\widetilde W^p)=2\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(W^p)$, and $\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(H_{\widetilde W})_-^\gamma=2\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(H_W)_-^\gamma$. Here conjugation identifies the spectra of $H_W$ and $H_{\overline W}$. The real inequality, divided by two, proves the result. ◻

</details>

<a id="comp:section"></a>
### From equality to pointwise matrix relations

We now apply Theorem <a href="#act:quantitative-action" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="act:quantitative-action">4</a> to finite collections of negative eigenfunctions. Equality forces each of its nonnegative errors to vanish. The main issue is that the number of eigenfunctions may grow without bound, whereas the potential has only $m$ channels. We will use this fixed number of columns to control the tails of the resulting matrices.

<a id="comp:equality"></a>
Throughout this section, $W$ is real symmetric, $W\geq0$ almost everywhere, and $0<\int_{\mathbb R}\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(W^p)<\infty$. Suppose that equality holds, in the form $$b_\sigma\sum_{i\in\mathcal I}k_i^{2\gamma}
   =D_\sigma\int_{\mathbb R}\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(W^p)\,dx,
 \label{comp:equality}$$ where $-k_i^2$ are the negative eigenvalues of $H_W$, repeated according to multiplicity, and $$k_1\geq k_2\geq\cdots>0.$$ By Lemma <a href="#pre:spectral" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:spectral">2</a>, the index set $\mathcal I$ is a nonempty finite initial segment of $\mathbb N$ or all of $\mathbb N$, each eigenvalue has finite multiplicity, and $k_i\to0$ in the infinite case.
