Fix real orthonormal eigenfunctions $\varphi_i\in H^1(\mathbb R;\mathbb R^m)$ and regard them as row vectors. Write $$\mathscr H=\ell^2(\mathcal I;\mathbb R),\qquad
 \Lambda=\operatorname{diag}(k_i:i\in\mathcal I),\qquad \kappa=k_1.$$ For operators between real Hilbert spaces, the superscript $T$ will also denote the adjoint.

<a id="stmt-comp-relations"></a>
<div id="comp:relations" class="proposition">

<a id="comp:row-eigenfunction"></a>
<a id="comp:potential-limit"></a>
**Proposition 7** (Pointwise relations at equality). *Under these assumptions, there are rows $a_i\in H^1(\mathbb R;\mathbb R^m)$, $i\in\mathcal I$, each a linear combination of eigenfunctions with eigenvalue $-k_i^2$, such that $$-a_i''-a_iW=-k_i^2a_i
 \quad\text{in distributions.}
 \label{comp:row-eigenfunction}$$ The rows $a_i$ are allowed to vanish. On a common set of full measure, they define a Hilbert–Schmidt operator $A(x):\mathbb R^m\to\mathscr H$, and there exists a bounded self-adjoint operator $B$ on $\mathscr H$ with $\|B\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}\leq\kappa$ such that <a id="comp:limit-relations"></a>$$=0,\qquad
 \Lambda^2-B^2=(AA^T)^r,\qquad
 a_i'(x)=(BA)_{i,\cdot}\quad(i\in\mathcal I).
 \label{comp:limit-relations}$$ Moreover, $$W(x)=q\bigl(A(x)^TA(x)\bigr)^r
 \quad\text{almost everywhere.}
 \label{comp:potential-limit}$$ The operator $B$ is asserted to exist separately at each such point $x$; no measurable choice is required.*

</div>

We divide the proof into the construction of a sequence whose errors vanish, convergence of every fixed row, and control of the remaining rows.

<a id="comp:approximation"></a>
#### Finite spectral approximations and vanishing errors

Let $n_j=j$ when $\mathcal I=\mathbb N$, and let $n_j=|\mathcal I|$ when $\mathcal I$ is finite. Choose $0<\xi_j<1/j$ so small that <a id="comp:common-shift"></a>$$\ell_{j,i}:=(k_i^2-\xi_j)^{1/2}>0\quad(1\leq i\leq n_j),
 \qquad
 \sum_{i\leq n_j}\bigl(k_i^{2\gamma}-\ell_{j,i}^{2\gamma}\bigr)<1/j.
 \label{comp:common-shift}$$ Set $K_j=\operatorname{diag}(\ell_{j,1},\ldots,\ell_{j,n_j})$. The same shift $\xi_j$ is used for every eigenvalue: this leaves the differences of their squares unchanged.

<a id="comp:form-approximation"></a>
The approximation leading to <a href="#bound:trial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="bound:trial">[bound:trial]</a> can be made arbitrarily accurate in $H^1$. Apply it to the first $n_j$ eigenfunctions, choosing the $H^1$ errors below $1/j$ and the form-matrix error below $\xi_j$. This gives real, smooth, compactly supported functions $u_{j,1},\ldots,u_{j,n_j}$, orthonormal in $L^2(\mathbb R;\mathbb R^m)$, such that $$\|u_{j,i}-\varphi_i\|_{H^1}<1/j,
 \qquad
 \bigl(h_W[u_{j,i},u_{j,l}]\bigr)_{i,l=1}^{n_j}\leq-K_j^2.
 \label{comp:form-approximation}$$ Here $h_W[\cdot,\cdot]$ is the polarized quadratic form, and the second inequality is the ordering of real symmetric matrices.

Let $U_j$ be the matrix with rows $u_{j,i}$, and choose a bounded interval $\Omega_j$ containing their supports in its interior. Apply Theorem <a href="#act:quantitative-action" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="act:quantitative-action">4</a> with $K=K_j$, $d=m$, $\kappa=k_1$, and $\eta=1/j$. It gives a lower triangular matrix $C_j$, a symmetric path $B_j$ with $\|B_j\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}\leq\kappa$, and $A_j=C_jU_j$. Extend $A_j$ by zero outside $\Omega_j$ and extend $B_j$ by $K_j$ to the left and by $-K_j$ to the right. All the errors in that theorem vanish outside $\Omega_j$, so their integrals may be taken over $\mathbb R$.

<a id="comp:triangular-gap"></a>
The lower triangular form of $C_j$ gives a useful additional error. Put $Z_j=A_j^TA_j$, an $m\times m$ positive semidefinite matrix. By <a href="#comp:form-approximation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:form-approximation">[comp:form-approximation]</a>, $$\mathcal E_{K_j}(A_j)
 \leq\int_{\mathbb R}\bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(WZ_j)-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(Z_j^q)\bigr)\,dx-T_j,
 \qquad
 T_j=\sum_{l\leq i\leq n_j}(C_j)_{il}^{\,2}(k_l^2-k_i^2)\geq0.
 \label{comp:triangular-gap}$$ Indeed, the $i$th row of $A_j$ is $\sum_{l\leq i}(C_j)_{il}u_{j,l}$. Its quadratic form, plus $\ell_{j,i}^2$ times its squared $L^2$ norm, is at most $$\sum_{l\leq i}(C_j)_{il}^{\,2}
      (\ell_{j,i}^2-\ell_{j,l}^2)
 =-\sum_{l\leq i}(C_j)_{il}^{\,2}(k_l^2-k_i^2).$$ Summing over $i$ proves <a href="#comp:triangular-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:triangular-gap">[comp:triangular-gap]</a>, since $A_jA_j^T$ and $Z_j$ have the same nonzero eigenvalues, with multiplicity. The ordering of the $k_i$ makes every summand of $T_j$ nonnegative.

<a id="comp:vanishing-errors"></a>
Recall the nonnegative Young deficit from Lemma <a href="#pre:young" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:young">3</a>: <a id="comp:young-deficit"></a>$$Y(W,Z)=D_\sigma\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(W^p)-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(WZ)+\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(Z^q)\geq0,
 \qquad
 Y(W,Z)=0\ \Longleftrightarrow\ W=qZ^r.
 \label{comp:young-deficit}$$ Combining Theorem <a href="#act:quantitative-action" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="act:quantitative-action">4</a>, <a href="#comp:equality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:equality">[comp:equality]</a>, and <a href="#comp:triangular-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:triangular-gap">[comp:triangular-gap]</a> yields $$\begin{aligned}
 0\leq T_j+\int_{\mathbb R}\Bigl(
   Y(W,Z_j)+\|A_j'-B_jA_j\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
       +c_*\Phi_{K_j}(A_j,B_j)\Bigr)\,dx
 &\leq d_j,\label{comp:vanishing-errors}\\
 d_j:=b_\sigma\left(
    \sum_{i\in\mathcal I}k_i^{2\gamma}
       -\sum_{i\leq n_j}\ell_{j,i}^{2\gamma}\right)+1/j
 &\longrightarrow0.\notag
\end{aligned}$$ Here $c_*>0$ is fixed: its independence of $n_j$ is essential. Thus all three integral errors tend to zero, as does $T_j$.

<a id="comp:coercivity"></a>
We will also need a uniform bound on $A_j$. The coercive estimate <a href="#pre:young-coercive" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pre:young-coercive">[pre:young-coercive]</a> gives $$Y(W,Z_j)\geq\frac12\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(Z_j^q)
       -(2^{p-1}-1)D_\sigma\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(W^p).
 \label{comp:coercivity}$$ Since $Z_j$ has size $m$, scalar Hölder’s inequality gives $$\|A_j\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2q}=(\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}Z_j)^q
       \leq m^{q-1}\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(Z_j^q).$$ Equations <a href="#comp:vanishing-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:vanishing-errors">[comp:vanishing-errors]</a> and <a href="#comp:coercivity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:coercivity">[comp:coercivity]</a> therefore bound $A_j$ uniformly in $L^{2q}(\mathbb R)$ with the Hilbert–Schmidt norm, independently of its number of rows.

<a id="comp:rows"></a>
#### Convergence of each fixed row

For fixed $i\in\mathcal I$, the coefficients $(C_j)_{il}$, $l\leq i$, are bounded as $j\to\infty$. To see this, choose a bounded interval $J_i$ so large that the restricted Gram matrix $$\left(\int_{J_i}\varphi_l(x)\varphi_s(x)^T\,dx
       \right)_{l,s=1}^{i}\geq\frac34 I_i,$$ where $I_i$ denotes the $i\times i$ identity matrix. Such an interval exists because the full Gram matrix is the identity. By <a href="#comp:form-approximation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:form-approximation">[comp:form-approximation]</a>, the corresponding restricted Gram matrix of the $u_{j,l}$ is at least one half of the identity for all sufficiently large $j$. Hence $$\frac12\sum_{l\leq i}(C_j)_{il}^{\,2}
 \leq\int_{J_i}|(A_j)_{i,\cdot}|^2\,dx
 \leq |J_i|^{1-1/q}
        \left(\int_{J_i}\|A_j\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2q}\,dx\right)^{1/q}.$$ The last expression is uniformly bounded. Lower triangularity is used here to keep the number of coefficients in a fixed row finite.

<a id="comp:gap-suppression"></a>
<a id="comp:row-convergence"></a>
A diagonal subsequence now makes every coefficient $(C_j)_{il}$ converge to a limit $c_{il}$, for $l\leq i$. From $T_j\to0$ and <a href="#comp:triangular-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:triangular-gap">[comp:triangular-gap]</a>, $$c_{il}=0\quad\text{whenever }k_l>k_i.
 \label{comp:gap-suppression}$$ Define $$a_i=\sum_{l\leq i}c_{il}\varphi_l.$$ The sum is finite, and <a href="#comp:form-approximation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:form-approximation">[comp:form-approximation]</a> implies $$(A_j)_{i,\cdot}\longrightarrow a_i
       \quad\text{in }H^1(\mathbb R;\mathbb R^m)
       \quad\text{for every fixed }i.
 \label{comp:row-convergence}$$ The ordering of the eigenvalues and <a href="#comp:gap-suppression" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:gap-suppression">[comp:gap-suppression]</a> show that only eigenfunctions with eigenvalue $-k_i^2$ occur in $a_i$. This proves <a href="#comp:row-eigenfunction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:row-eigenfunction">[comp:row-eigenfunction]</a>, including the possibility $a_i=0$. The eigenfunction equations hold distributionally by testing the form equations against smooth compactly supported functions; the potential is locally integrable and the rows are locally bounded.

<a id="comp:pointwise-errors"></a>
Choose the continuous, locally absolutely continuous representatives of these rows. The Sobolev embedding $H^1(\mathbb R)\subset C_b(\mathbb R)$ shows that <a href="#comp:row-convergence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:row-convergence">[comp:row-convergence]</a> also gives pointwise convergence at every $x$. Passing to a further subsequence, we may arrange that the integrals of the nonnegative integrand in <a href="#comp:vanishing-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:vanishing-errors">[comp:vanishing-errors]</a> are summable, and that, for every fixed $i$, the squared $L^2$ errors of the derivatives in <a href="#comp:row-convergence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:row-convergence">[comp:row-convergence]</a> are summable. A diagonal choice achieves both conditions, by making the first $j$ available derivative errors and the integral error less than $2^{-j}$ along the chosen subsequence. Consequently, on a common set of full measure, $$\begin{gathered}
 Y(W,Z_j)\to0,\qquad
 \|A_j'-B_jA_j\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\to0,\qquad
 \Phi_{K_j}(A_j,B_j)\to0,\\
 (A_j')_{i,\cdot}(x)\to a_i'(x)
       \quad\text{for every }i\in\mathcal I.
 \end{gathered}
 \label{comp:pointwise-errors}$$ We also exclude the null set where $W$ is not a finite positive semidefinite matrix. It remains to show that convergence of the individual rows is strong enough to pass to the products in these relations.

<a id="comp:tails"></a>
#### Tail control in the row-index space

<a id="comp:diagonal-limit"></a>
Pad $A_j$, $B_j$, and $K_j$ with zeros in the unused coordinates of $\mathscr H$. Thus $A_j:\mathbb R^m\to\mathscr H$, and $B_j,K_j$ are self-adjoint finite-rank operators on $\mathscr H$. Since the omitted $k_i$ tend to zero, $$\|K_j^2-\Lambda^2\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}
    \leq\max\{\xi_j,\sup_{i>n_j}k_i^2\}\longrightarrow0,
 \label{comp:diagonal-limit}$$ where a supremum over no indices is zero.

<a id="comp:pointwise-algebra-error"></a>
<a id="comp:tail-bounds"></a>
Fix a point $x$ in the full-measure set from <a href="#comp:pointwise-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:pointwise-errors">[comp:pointwise-errors]</a> and suppress $x$ for the moment. Write $\rho_j=A_jA_j^T$. The definition of $\Phi$ and its convergence to zero give $$\|[B_j,K_j^2]\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}\to0,
 \qquad e_j:=\|K_j^2-B_j^2-\rho_j^r\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}\to0.
 \label{comp:pointwise-algebra-error}$$ For $h\geq1$, let $P_h$ be the orthogonal projection onto the first $h$ coordinates of $\mathscr H$, with all coordinates included if $h$ exceeds $|\mathcal I|$. Set $Q_h=I-P_h$ and $s_h=\sup_{i>h}k_i^2$, again with the empty supremum equal to zero. Then $s_h\to0$. For every unit vector $v$ in the range of $Q_h$, <a id="comp:tail-quadratic"></a>$$\|B_jv\|^2+\langle v,\rho_j^rv\rangle\leq s_h+e_j.
 \label{comp:tail-quadratic}$$ Both terms on the left are nonnegative. Jensen’s inequality for the spectral measure of $\rho_j$, using $r>1$, implies $$\langle v,\rho_jv\rangle^r\leq\langle v,\rho_j^rv\rangle.$$ It follows that $\|Q_h\rho_jQ_h\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}\leq(s_h+e_j)^{1/r}$. Although $\mathscr H$ may be infinite dimensional, $Q_h\rho_jQ_h$ has rank at most $m$, because $A_j$ has exactly $m$ columns. Its trace is therefore at most $m$ times its operator norm. We obtain the two tail bounds $$\boxed{\quad
 \|Q_hA_j\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\leq m(s_h+e_j)^{1/r},
 \qquad
 \|B_jQ_h\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}\leq(s_h+e_j)^{1/2}.
 \quad}
 \label{comp:tail-bounds}$$ The first estimate converts decay of the eigenvalue parameters into decay of the total mass of all omitted rows. Its factor is the fixed physical dimension $m$, rather than the number of retained eigenfunctions; Figure <a href="#comp:tail-figure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="comp:tail-figure">1</a> depicts this distinction.

<figure id="comp:tail-figure">

<figcaption>The number of rows can grow with the number of negative eigenfunctions, but the number of physical channels remains $m$. After fixing the first $h$ rows, the identity $K_{j}^{2} ≈ B_{j}^{2} + (A_{j}A_{j}^{T})^{r}$ bounds the operator norm in the remaining rows. The rank bound converts it into a Hilbert–Schmidt bound, which tends to zero as first $j → ∞$ and then $h → ∞$. The drawing is schematic; all matrices are padded by zero rows in $ℋ$.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-comp-tail-figure"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`comp:tail-figure`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
% definitions used by this figure, copied from the source preamble
\newcommand{\HS}{\mathrm{HS}}
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\small,>=Latex]
  \fill[black!5] (0,2.05) rectangle (2.7,3.65);
  \fill[blue!7] (0,0) rectangle (2.7,2.05);
  \draw[thick] (0,0) rectangle (2.7,3.65);
  \draw[thick] (0,2.05)--(2.7,2.05);
  \draw[<->] (0,4.05)--node[above]{$m$ columns, fixed}(2.7,4.05);
  \node at (1.35,2.83) {$P_hA_j$};
  \node at (1.35,1.28) {$Q_hA_j$};
  \node at (1.35,0.55) {$\vdots$};
  \node[anchor=east,align=right] at (-0.2,2.83)
       {first $h$\\rows};
  \node[anchor=east,align=right] at (-0.2,1.08)
       {all remaining\\rows};
  \draw[->] (2.9,2.83)--(3.6,2.83);
  \node[anchor=west,align=left] at (3.75,2.83)
       {Finite-dimensional convergence\\for each fixed $h$};
  \draw[->] (2.9,1.08)--(3.6,1.08);
  \node[anchor=west,align=left] at (3.75,1.08)
       {$\operatorname{rank}(Q_hA_j)\leq m$\\[4pt]
        $\|Q_hA_j\|_{\HS}^2\leq m(s_h+e_j)^{1/r}$};
 \end{tikzpicture}
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 14 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
Schematic diagram showing a tall rectangular matrix partitioned horizontally. Above the rectangle is a two-sided horizontal arrow labeled "$m\text{ columns, fixed}$". To the left, the top partition is labeled "first $h$ rows" and contains the expression "$P_h A_j$"; a horizontal arrow points from this block to the text "Finite-dimensional convergence for each fixed $h$". The bottom partition is labeled to the left as "all remaining rows" and contains "$Q_h A_j$" followed by vertical dots ($\vdots$); a curly bracket to its right groups the inequalities "$\operatorname{rank}(Q_h A_j) \le m$" and "$\|Q_h A_j\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2 \le m(s_h + e_j)^{1/r}$".
```


For each fixed $h$, the first $h$ rows of $A_j$ converge by <a href="#comp:row-convergence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:row-convergence">[comp:row-convergence]</a>. The first bound in <a href="#comp:tail-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:tail-bounds">[comp:tail-bounds]</a> makes the remaining rows uniformly small by choosing $h$ large and then $j$ sufficiently large. Thus $A_j$ is Cauchy in Hilbert–Schmidt norm and converges to an operator $A:\mathbb R^m\to\mathscr H$ whose rows are precisely $a_i(x)$: <a id="comp:HS-limit"></a>$$A_j\longrightarrow A\quad\text{in Hilbert--Schmidt norm.}
 \label{comp:HS-limit}$$ This argument applies to the whole subsequence already selected and does not require a further choice depending on $x$.

For $B_j$ we need only pointwise compactness. Its finite compressions $P_hB_jP_h$ are bounded in finite-dimensional spaces, so a diagonal subsequence makes them converge for every $h$. Self-adjointness and the second estimate in <a href="#comp:tail-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:tail-bounds">[comp:tail-bounds]</a> give $$\|B_j-P_hB_jP_h\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}
   \leq2\|B_jQ_h\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}
   \leq2(s_h+e_j)^{1/2}.$$ The chosen subsequence consequently converges in operator norm to a self-adjoint $B$ with $\|B\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}\leq\kappa$. This subsequence, and hence $B$, may depend on the point $x$.

We can now pass to all the required products. Hilbert–Schmidt convergence of $A_j$ implies operator norm convergence of $A_jA_j^T$ and of $A_j^TA_j$. Continuous functional calculus for nonnegative operators then gives convergence of their $r$th powers. Together with <a href="#comp:diagonal-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:diagonal-limit">[comp:diagonal-limit]</a> and <a href="#comp:pointwise-algebra-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:pointwise-algebra-error">[comp:pointwise-algebra-error]</a>, this proves $$[B,\Lambda^2]=0,\qquad
 \Lambda^2-B^2=(AA^T)^r.$$ Also $B_jA_j\to BA$ in Hilbert–Schmidt norm. Taking its $i$th row and using both the derivative convergence and the first-order error in <a href="#comp:pointwise-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:pointwise-errors">[comp:pointwise-errors]</a> gives $a_i'(x)=(BA)_{i,\cdot}$ for every $i$. Finally, continuity of the finite-dimensional Young deficit and $Y(W,A_j^TA_j)\to0$ yield $Y(W,A^TA)=0$. Lemma <a href="#pre:young" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:young">3</a> gives $W=q(A^TA)^r$.

We have proved these statements at every point of one common set of full measure. The construction selects no function $x\mapsto B(x)$: only the existence of a suitable operator at each such point enters the next section. This completes the proof of Proposition <a href="#comp:relations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="comp:relations">7</a>.

<a id="rig:section"></a>
### Fixed channels and the equality profiles

The pointwise relations of Proposition <a href="#comp:relations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="comp:relations">7</a> still involve an auxiliary operator $B$ that may depend on the point without a prescribed measurable choice. We now extract constant subspaces from those relations. On each nonzero subspace, $B$ becomes a uniquely determined matrix and satisfies an ordinary differential equation. This will identify every real equality potential; realification and a scalar calculation will then complete the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### The channel subspaces are constant

Assume first that $W$ is real and is a nonzero equality potential. Use the rows $a_i$, numbers $k_i$, and operator $\Lambda=\operatorname{diag}(k_i)$ from Proposition <a href="#comp:relations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="comp:relations">7</a>. For each distinct value $\alpha>0$ in the list $(k_i)$, let $n_\alpha$ be its finite multiplicity, and let $G_\alpha$ be the $n_\alpha\times m$ matrix whose rows are the $a_i$ with $k_i=\alpha$. Each $G_\alpha$ has entries in $H^1(\mathbb R)$, and we use their continuous representatives.

<a id="rig:orthogonal-blocks"></a>
<a id="rig:block-first-order"></a>
<a id="rig:block-square"></a>
At every point in the common full-measure set of that proposition, choose one of the operators $B$ it supplies. Since $B$ commutes with $\Lambda^2$, it preserves each of its eigenspaces. Moreover, $$AA^T=(\Lambda^2-B^2)^{1/r}$$ commutes with both $B$ and $\Lambda^2$. Writing $B_\alpha$ for the block on the $\alpha^2$-eigenspace therefore gives $$\begin{aligned}
 G_\alpha G_\beta^T&=0 &&(\alpha\ne\beta),
 \label{rig:orthogonal-blocks}\\
 G_\alpha'&=B_\alpha G_\alpha,
 & [B_\alpha,G_\alpha G_\alpha^T]&=0,
 \label{rig:block-first-order}\\
 \alpha^2I-B_\alpha^2&=(G_\alpha G_\alpha^T)^r,
 &\|B_\alpha\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}&\le\kappa.
 \label{rig:block-square}
\end{aligned}$$ These statements hold almost everywhere and require no compatibility between the choices of $B$ at different points.

<a id="stmt-rig-fixed-right-spaces"></a>
<div id="rig:fixed-right-spaces" class="lemma">

<a id="rig:dimension-bound"></a>
**Lemma 8**. *For every $\alpha$, the subspace $$\mathcal R_\alpha=(\ker G_\alpha(x))^\perp\subset\mathbb R^m$$ is independent of $x\in\mathbb R$. The nonzero spaces $\mathcal R_\alpha$ are mutually orthogonal, and $$\label{rig:dimension-bound}
 \sum_\alpha\dim\mathcal R_\alpha\le m.$$ In particular, only finitely many $G_\alpha$ are nonzero. The potential $W$ has a continuous representative, equal to zero on the orthogonal complement of these spaces and equal to $q(G_\alpha^TG_\alpha)^r$ on $\mathcal R_\alpha$. With this representative, every $G_\alpha$ is $C^2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rig-fixed-right-spaces"><summary>Proof of stmt-rig-fixed-right-spaces</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $v\in\mathbb R^m$. Equation <a href="#rig:block-first-order" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rig:block-first-order">[rig:block-first-order]</a> implies $$\|(G_\alpha v)'(x)\|\le\kappa\|G_\alpha(x)v\|
 \quad\text{for almost every }x.$$ The function $G_\alpha v$ is locally absolutely continuous. If it vanishes at $x_0$, integration and Gronwall’s inequality, first to the right and then to the left of $x_0$, show that it vanishes everywhere. Consequently $\ker G_\alpha(x)$ is independent of $x$, including points outside the original full-measure set. This proves the first assertion.

The products in <a href="#rig:orthogonal-blocks" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rig:orthogonal-blocks">[rig:orthogonal-blocks]</a> are continuous, so their almost-everywhere vanishing implies their vanishing everywhere. Thus the constant row spaces $\mathcal R_\alpha$ are mutually orthogonal. Any finite collection has total dimension at most $m$, proving <a href="#rig:dimension-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rig:dimension-bound">[rig:dimension-bound]</a> and finiteness of the nonzero blocks.

<a id="rig:continuous-potential"></a>
<a id="rig:block-second-order"></a>
On the full-measure set from Proposition <a href="#comp:relations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="comp:relations">7</a>, $A^TA=\sum_\alpha G_\alpha^TG_\alpha$. Each summand acts on its own orthogonal space $\mathcal R_\alpha$, and only finitely many summands are nonzero. Equation <a href="#comp:potential-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:potential-limit">[comp:potential-limit]</a> therefore yields $$\label{rig:continuous-potential}
 W=q\sum_\alpha(G_\alpha^TG_\alpha)^r
 \quad\text{almost everywhere}.$$ The right-hand side is continuous and has the asserted restrictions; we henceforth use it as the representative of $W$. The row eigenfunction equation <a href="#comp:row-eigenfunction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:row-eigenfunction">[comp:row-eigenfunction]</a> now reads $$\label{rig:block-second-order}
 G_\alpha''=\alpha^2G_\alpha-G_\alpha W
 \quad\text{in distributions}.$$ Its right-hand side is continuous. Integrating this identity twice shows that the continuous representative of $G_\alpha$ is $C^2$. ◻

</details>

The row spaces already split the physical space $\mathbb R^m$ into finitely many constant channels. To determine the potential within one of them, we also need a constant range for $G_\alpha$ in the eigenfunction index space $\mathbb R^{n_\alpha}$.

<a id="stmt-rig-fixed-left-spaces"></a>
<div id="rig:fixed-left-spaces" class="lemma">

<a id="rig:active-identities"></a>
**Lemma 9**. *For each $\alpha$ with $\ell:=\dim\mathcal R_\alpha>0$, the range of $G_\alpha(x)$ in $\mathbb R^{n_\alpha}$ is independent of $x$. In constant orthonormal bases of this range and of $\mathcal R_\alpha$, the restriction of $G_\alpha$ is an invertible $C^2$ matrix $D:\mathbb R\to\mathbb R^{\ell\times\ell}$. The matrix $B_0=D'D^{-1}$ is $C^1$ and satisfies, everywhere on $\mathbb R$, $$\label{rig:active-identities}
 B_0=B_0^T,\qquad
 \alpha^2I-B_0^2=(DD^T)^r>0,\qquad
 B_0'=r(B_0^2-\alpha^2I).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rig-fixed-left-spaces"><summary>Proof of stmt-rig-fixed-left-spaces</summary>

*Proof.* Choose a constant orthonormal basis of $\mathcal R_\alpha$, and let $E(x)$ be the matrix of the restriction of $G_\alpha(x)$ to this space. It has full column rank $\ell$ at every point, since the kernel of $G_\alpha$ is constant. The projection onto its range is $$P(x)=E(x)(E(x)^TE(x))^{-1}E(x)^T.$$ The commutator in <a href="#rig:block-first-order" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rig:block-first-order">[rig:block-first-order]</a> shows that $B_\alpha$ preserves the range of $G_\alpha G_\alpha^T$, which is precisely the range of $E$. Hence $E'=B_\alpha E$ has its columns in this range almost everywhere. Differentiating the projection gives $$\begin{aligned}
 P'={}&(I-P)E'(E^TE)^{-1}E^T\\
     &+E(E^TE)^{-1}(E')^T(I-P)=0
 \quad\text{almost everywhere}.
\end{aligned}$$ Since $P$ is $C^1$, it is constant.

Choose a constant orthonormal basis of this range as well. The resulting square matrix $D$ is invertible everywhere and $C^2$. Almost everywhere, $D'D^{-1}$ is the restriction of $B_\alpha$ to its invariant range in these coordinates. Equations <a href="#rig:block-first-order" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rig:block-first-order">[rig:block-first-order]</a> and <a href="#rig:block-square" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rig:block-square">[rig:block-square]</a> give the symmetry and square identity in <a href="#rig:active-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rig:active-identities">[rig:active-identities]</a> almost everywhere; continuity extends them to every point. Positivity is strict because $D$ is invertible.

On $\mathcal R_\alpha$, Equation <a href="#rig:continuous-potential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rig:continuous-potential">[rig:continuous-potential]</a> identifies the potential with $q(D^TD)^r$. Equation  <a href="#rig:block-second-order" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rig:block-second-order">[rig:block-second-order]</a> consequently becomes $$D''=\alpha^2D-qD(D^TD)^r
     =\alpha^2D-q(DD^T)^rD.$$ The second equality follows, for example, by a singular-value decomposition. Differentiating $B_0=D'D^{-1}$ and using $q=r+1$ now gives $$B_0'=D''D^{-1}-B_0^2
      =\alpha^2I-q(\alpha^2I-B_0^2)-B_0^2
      =r(B_0^2-\alpha^2I),$$ as claimed. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### The matrix Riccati equation separates the profiles

Fix a nonzero block and the matrices of Lemma <a href="#rig:fixed-left-spaces" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rig:fixed-left-spaces">9</a>. Diagonalize the symmetric matrix $B_0(x_*)$ at one point by a constant orthogonal change of range coordinates. The right-hand side of its Riccati equation is a polynomial in $B_0$. Uniqueness for this matrix ordinary differential equation therefore preserves the diagonal form. More explicitly, the strict inequality in <a href="#rig:active-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rig:active-identities">[rig:active-identities]</a> places each initial diagonal entry in $(-\alpha,\alpha)$. There is a unique $y_j\in\mathbb R$ such that the scalar solution with that initial value is <a id="rig:tanh"></a>$$\label{rig:tanh}
 b_j(x)=-\alpha\tanh\bigl(r\alpha(x-y_j)\bigr),
 \qquad 1\le j\le\ell.$$ The diagonal matrix with these entries solves the same initial-value problem as $B_0$ on all of $\mathbb R$, so uniqueness gives equality everywhere.

<a id="rig:profiles"></a>
Equation <a href="#rig:active-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rig:active-identities">[rig:active-identities]</a> implies that $DD^T$ is diagonal in these coordinates. Since $D'=\operatorname{diag}(b_j)D$, each row of $D$ is a positive scalar multiple of its value at $x_*$. None of these rows is zero, and their directions are mutually orthogonal because $DD^T$ is diagonal. Taking their normalized directions as a constant orthonormal basis of $\mathcal R_\alpha$ makes $D$ diagonal with positive entries. The potential in this fixed basis is therefore diagonal, with entries $$\label{rig:profiles}
 q\bigl(\alpha^2-b_j(x)^2\bigr)
 =(r+1)\alpha^2\operatorname{sech}^2\bigl(r\alpha(x-y_j)\bigr).$$ Combining these bases over the finitely many nonzero spaces $\mathcal R_\alpha$, and adding a basis of their orthogonal complement, proves the required necessity for real $W$. There are at most $m$ nonzero profiles by <a href="#rig:dimension-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rig:dimension-bound">[rig:dimension-bound]</a>. The zero potential has the same description with no nonzero profile.

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Complex Hermitian potentials

For a complex Hermitian potential $W$, take the realification $\widetilde W$ from <a href="#bound:realification" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="bound:realification">[bound:realification]</a>. The proof of Proposition <a href="#bound:sharp" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="bound:sharp">6</a> shows that it is equivalent to $W\oplus\overline W$ by a constant unitary matrix and that both the trace-power integral and the negative spectral moment are doubled. Thus equality for $W$ implies equality for the real potential $\widetilde W$.

The real result provides a constant unitary matrix $V$ such that $$W(x)\oplus\overline W(x)
   =V\operatorname{diag}(f_1(x),\ldots,f_{2m}(x))V^*
 \quad\text{almost everywhere},$$ where each $f_j$ is either a profile of the form <a href="#rig:profiles" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rig:profiles">[rig:profiles]</a> or zero. Let $P$ be the projection onto the first summand $\mathbb C^m$ and put $\widehat P=V^*PV$. Since $P$ commutes with $W\oplus\overline W$, $$(f_i(x)-f_j(x))\widehat P_{ij}=0
 \quad\text{almost everywhere}.$$ Consequently $\widehat P_{ij}=0$ whenever the functions $f_i,f_j$ are not equal almost everywhere. Partition the indices into groups of identical functions, including the possible zero function. The range of $\widehat P$ splits as the orthogonal sum of its intersections with these coordinate groups. On each group the diagonal potential is a scalar multiple of the identity, so any constant orthonormal basis of that intersection diagonalizes its restriction. Transporting these bases by $V$ to the first summand gives a constant unitary basis of $\mathbb C^m$ in which $W$ has the asserted profiles. This proves necessity for complex Hermitian potentials.

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### Each listed potential attains equality

<a id="rig:factorization"></a>
<a id="rig:partner"></a>
It remains to verify attainment, including the exact spectral moment, without appealing to a classification of scalar optimizers. Fix $a>0$ and $x_0\in\mathbb R$, and set $$V(x)=(r+1)a^2\operatorname{sech}^2\bigl(ra(x-x_0)\bigr),
 \qquad H=-\partial_x^2-V.$$ The first-order factorization used in the commutation argument of <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[BenguriaLoss2000](#ref-BenguriaLoss2000), Section II] also explains why this profile has only one negative eigenvalue. Put $$w(x)=a\tanh\bigl(ra(x-x_0)\bigr),
 \qquad Q=\partial_x+w,\qquad \operatorname{Dom}Q=H^1(\mathbb R).$$ Then $Q^*=-\partial_x+w$ on $H^1(\mathbb R)$. Since $w$ and $w'$ are bounded, both product domains are $H^2(\mathbb R)$, and direct differentiation yields $$\begin{aligned}
 Q^*Q&=H+a^2,
 \label{rig:factorization}\\
 QQ^*&=-\partial_x^2+a^2
       +(r-1)a^2\operatorname{sech}^2\bigl(ra(x-x_0)\bigr)
       \ge a^2.
 \label{rig:partner}
\end{aligned}$$ Here the last inequality uses $r>1$. The equation $Q\psi=0$ has the one-dimensional $L^2$ solution space $$\ker Q=
 \operatorname{span}\left\{
    \operatorname{sech}^{1/r}\bigl(ra(x-x_0)\bigr)
 \right\}.$$ Thus $-a^2$ is a simple eigenvalue of $H$, and <a href="#rig:factorization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rig:factorization">[rig:factorization]</a> excludes eigenvalues below it. If $H\phi=\lambda\phi$ with $-a^2<\lambda<0$, put $\mu=\lambda+a^2$ and $g=Q\phi$. Then $\|g\|_2^2=\mu\|\phi\|_2^2>0$. Moreover, $Q^*Q\phi=\mu\phi\in\operatorname{Dom}Q$, so $g\in\operatorname{Dom}(QQ^*)$ and $QQ^*g=\mu g$. This contradicts <a href="#rig:partner" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="rig:partner">[rig:partner]</a>, since $\mu<a^2$. By Lemma <a href="#pre:spectral" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:spectral">2</a>, all negative spectrum is discrete. Hence $-a^2$ is the sole negative eigenvalue.

Finally, $2p-1=2\gamma$ and the substitution $s=\tanh y$ give $$\begin{aligned}
 \int_\mathbb RV(x)^p\,dx
 &=\frac{(r+1)^p}{r}a^{2\gamma}
     \int_\mathbb R\operatorname{sech}^{2p}y\,dy\\
 &=\frac{(r+1)^p}{r}a^{2\gamma}
     \int_{-1}^{1}(1-s^2)^{p-1}\,ds
  =\frac{b_\sigma}{D_\sigma}a^{2\gamma},
\end{aligned}$$ where $(r+1)^p/r=D_\sigma^{-1}$ and $p-1=\sigma$. Consequently $$\operatorname{Tr}(H)_-^\gamma
   =a^{2\gamma}
   =C_\gamma\int_\mathbb RV^p.$$ The zero potential contributes zero to both sides. Finite orthogonal direct sums add the spectral moments and trace-power integrals, and a constant unitary conjugation leaves both unchanged. Every potential listed in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> therefore attains equality, completing its proof.

<a id="app:trace"></a>
### The exact integrated trace deficit

<a id="trace:normalization"></a>
<a id="trace:scaling"></a>
The matrix comparison needed in the action argument has an exact remainder. We give the proof from <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[MLT](#ref-MLT), Section 3], retaining both nonnegative terms. Together they control the commutator and the algebraic equality relation used in the action argument. Throughout this appendix, $0<\sigma<1$, $\beta=\sigma-\tfrac12$, and $r=1/\sigma$, $p=1+\sigma$, $q=1+r$. Define $c_\sigma>0$ by $$\label{trace:normalization}
 c_\sigma^{-1}
 =\int_0^\infty t^\beta
       \bigl(t^{-1/2}-(t+1)^{-1/2}\bigr)\,dt.$$ The integrand is $O(t^{\sigma-1})$ at zero and $O(t^{\sigma-2})$ at infinity. Scaling therefore gives $$\label{trace:scaling}
 c_\sigma\int_0^\infty t^\beta
       \bigl(t^{-1/2}-(t+y)^{-1/2}\bigr)\,dt=y^\sigma,
 \qquad y\geq0.$$

<a id="stmt-trace-deficit"></a>
<div id="trace:deficit" class="theorem">

<a id="trace:symbol"></a>
<a id="trace:resolvents"></a>
<a id="trace:N"></a>
<a id="trace:LZ"></a>
<a id="trace:deficit-identity"></a>
**Theorem 10** (Exact trace deficit). *Let $n\geq1$ and let $B,V\in\mathbb C^{n\times n}$ be Hermitian. Suppose that $$\label{trace:symbol}
 Y_s=(sI-B)^2,\qquad X_s=Y_s+V\geq bI
 \quad(s\in\mathbb R)$$ for some $b>0$. Set $$\label{trace:resolvents}
 R(t)=\frac1\pi\int_\mathbb R(X_s+tI)^{-1}\,ds,
 \qquad P(t)=t^{-1/2}I-R(t),\qquad t>0,$$ and $$\label{trace:N}
 N=c_\sigma\int_0^\infty t^\beta P(t)\,dt.$$ These integrals converge absolutely in matrix norm, and $R(t)>0$. There are continuous Hermitian matrices $L(t)=R(t)^{-1}>0$ and $Z(t)$ satisfying $$\label{trace:LZ}
 V+tI=L^2+Z^2+i[B,Z],\qquad
 LZ+ZL+i[B,L]=0.$$ If $N\geq0$, then $$\label{trace:deficit-identity}
 \begin{split}
 \mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(NV)-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(N^q)
 =c_\sigma\int_0^\infty t^\beta\Bigl(
    \mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(RZ^2)
    +q\bigl\|R^{-1/2}-R^{1/2}(tI+N^r)^{1/2}\bigr\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
    \Bigr)\,dt.
 \end{split}$$ In particular, $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(NV)\geq\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(N^q)$.*

</div>

The hypothesis concerns the full symbol $X_s$; the perturbation $V$ itself need not be positive. The proof first recovers $L$ and $Z$ from solutions on the two half-lines, then evaluates a logarithmic integral, and finally completes a matrix square.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### Half-line boundary maps

<a id="trace:P-bounds"></a>
Quadratic growth in $s$, together with <a href="#trace:symbol" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="trace:symbol">[trace:symbol]</a>, gives an integrable bound for $X_s^{-1}$. Hence $R(t)>0$ and $R(t)=O(1)$ as $t\downarrow0$. Diagonalization of $B$ gives $$\frac1\pi\int_\mathbb R(Y_s+tI)^{-1}\,ds=t^{-1/2}I.$$ For $t\geq1$, both $X_s+tI$ and $Y_s+tI$ dominate $c(s^2+t)I$, with $c>0$ independent of $s,t$. The resolvent identity bounds their inverse difference by $C(s^2+t)^{-2}$, and therefore $$\label{trace:P-bounds}
 \|P(t)\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}=
 \begin{cases}
  O(t^{-1/2}),&t\downarrow0,\\
  O(t^{-3/2}),&t\to\infty.
 \end{cases}$$ These bounds prove the asserted convergence of $N$.

<a id="trace:ode"></a>
Fix $t>0$ and put $\nabla=\partial_x-iB$. We shall use the equation $$\label{trace:ode}
 (-\nabla^2+V+tI)\psi=0.$$ Matrix Riccati equations also appear in the commutation method for Schrödinger inequalities <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[BenguriaLoss2000](#ref-BenguriaLoss2000)]. Here their role is to compute the integrated resolvent through boundary data.

For each $v\in\mathbb C^n$ there is a unique $H^1$ solution of <a href="#trace:ode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="trace:ode">[trace:ode]</a> on either half-line with $\psi(0)=v$. Indeed, the sesquilinear form $$a_t(\phi,\psi)=\int
  \bigl(\langle\nabla\phi,\nabla\psi\rangle
       +\langle\phi,(V+tI)\psi\rangle\bigr)\,dx,$$ with inner products linear in the second variable, is coercive on $H^1(\mathbb R;\mathbb C^n)$: its Fourier symbol $X_s+tI$ dominates $c_t(1+s^2)I$. Zero extension gives coercivity on the trace-zero subspace of either half-line. Correcting any $H^1$ extension of $v$ by the Riesz representation theorem gives the unique weak solution. The equation implies $\psi''\in L^2$, so this solution lies in $H^2$; both $\psi$ and $\psi'$ vanish at the infinite end.

<a id="trace:riccati"></a>
Define the right and left boundary matrices $S,T$ by <a id="trace:boundary"></a>$$\label{trace:boundary}
 \nabla\psi(0+)=-Sv,\qquad \nabla\psi(0-)=Tv.$$ Integration by parts gives $a_t(\psi_v,\psi_w)=\langle v,Sw\rangle$ on the right and $a_t(\psi_v,\psi_w)=\langle v,Tw\rangle$ on the left. Thus $S,T$ are Hermitian. Translation and uniqueness show that $\nabla\psi=-S\psi$ throughout the right half-line and $\nabla\psi=T\psi$ throughout the left half-line. Substituting these relations into <a href="#trace:ode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="trace:ode">[trace:ode]</a>, and allowing arbitrary boundary data, gives $$\label{trace:riccati}
 V+tI=S^2+i[B,S]=T^2-i[B,T].$$

Glue the two solutions with boundary value $v$ at zero. The resulting continuous function has derivative jump $-(S+T)v$, and hence satisfies $$(-\nabla^2+V+tI)\psi=\delta_0(S+T)v.$$ Fourier inversion, with forward kernel $e^{-isx}$, gives $$v=\frac1{2\pi}\int_\mathbb R(X_s+tI)^{-1}\,ds\,(S+T)v
   =\tfrac12 R(t)(S+T)v.$$ The inverse symbol is $O(s^{-2})$, so its Fourier inverse is continuous and this evaluation at zero is justified. Therefore $$L:=\tfrac12(S+T)=R^{-1}>0,\qquad Z:=\tfrac12(S-T)$$ satisfy <a href="#trace:LZ" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="trace:LZ">[trace:LZ]</a>, by adding and subtracting <a href="#trace:riccati" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="trace:riccati">[trace:riccati]</a>. The second equation in <a href="#trace:LZ" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="trace:LZ">[trace:LZ]</a> determines $Z$ uniquely from $L$, since $L>0$; in an eigenbasis of $L$, the map $Z\mapsto LZ+ZL$ multiplies its $(j,k)$ entry by the positive number $L_{jj}+L_{kk}$. This also proves the continuity of $Z(t)$, because $R(t)$, and hence $L(t)$, is continuous.

<a id="trace:RV"></a>
The diagonal entries of that same equation give $Z_{jj}=0$ in an eigenbasis of $L$, so $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}Z=0$. Multiplication on both sides by $R=L^{-1}$ gives $i[R,B]=-(ZR+RZ)$. Cyclicity of the trace now yields $$\label{trace:RV}
 \mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl(R(V+tI)\bigr)=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}L-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(RZ^2).$$ In particular, the correction $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(RZ^2)=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(ZRZ)$ is nonnegative. We will also need the location of the spectrum of $B+iS$. The right solutions obey $\psi'=i(B+iS)\psi$. For an eigenvector with eigenvalue $z$, the solution is $e^{izx}v$; its square integrability forces $\operatorname{Im}z>0$.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### A logarithmic integral

<a id="trace:F"></a>
The next step converts the boundary matrices into a scalar identity that can be integrated in $t$. Define $$\label{trace:F}
 F(t)=\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}V}{\sqrt t}
       -\frac1\pi\int_\mathbb R
          \log\frac{\det(X_s+tI)}{\det(Y_s+tI)}\,ds.$$ The determinants are positive, and the logarithmic ratio is $O(|s|^{-2})$ at infinity. Its derivative in $t$ is the trace of the resolvent difference, with an integrable bound locally uniform for $t>0$. Thus the integral converges absolutely and $F$ is continuously differentiable.

<a id="trace:F-evaluation"></a>
The first identity in <a href="#trace:riccati" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="trace:riccati">[trace:riccati]</a> factors the symbol as $$X_s+tI=(sI-B+iS)(sI-B-iS).$$ If $u_j+iv_j$ are the eigenvalues of $B+iS$, counted with algebraic multiplicity, then $v_j>0$ and $$\det(X_s+tI)=\prod_{j=1}^n\bigl((s-u_j)^2+v_j^2\bigr).$$ The denominator in <a href="#trace:F" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="trace:F">[trace:F]</a> has the same form, with centers the eigenvalues of $B$ and widths $\sqrt t$. Translations of the individual logarithms change their symmetric-cutoff integrals by $o(1)$: for $h(s)=\log(s^2+v^2)$, $$\frac{d}{da}\int_{-M}^M h(s-a)\,ds
       =h(M+a)-h(M-a)\longrightarrow0$$ uniformly for bounded $a$. After centering the factors, the identity $$\int_\mathbb R\log\frac{s^2+v^2}{s^2+w^2}\,ds=2\pi(v-w),
 \qquad v,w>0,$$ follows by differentiation in $v$ and integration from $w$ to $v$. Since $\sum_jv_j=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}S=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}L$, we obtain $$\label{trace:F-evaluation}
 F(t)=\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}V}{\sqrt t}-2\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(L-\sqrt t I).$$

<a id="trace:G"></a>
<a id="trace:FG"></a>
Set $$\label{trace:G}
 G(t)=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(L-2\sqrt t I+tR).$$ Both functions are nonnegative. For $G$, apply $\lambda-2\sqrt t+t/\lambda\geq0$ to the positive eigenvalues $\lambda$ of $L$. For $F$, take the trace of <a href="#trace:LZ" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="trace:LZ">[trace:LZ]</a> and use <a href="#trace:F-evaluation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="trace:F-evaluation">[trace:F-evaluation]</a> to obtain $$F(t)=t^{-1/2}\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl((L-\sqrt t I)^2+Z^2\bigr)\geq0.$$ Equations <a href="#trace:RV" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="trace:RV">[trace:RV]</a> and <a href="#trace:F-evaluation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="trace:F-evaluation">[trace:F-evaluation]</a> give the identity $$\label{trace:FG}
 \mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(PV)=F+G+\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(RZ^2).$$ Every term on the right is nonnegative. The bounds <a href="#trace:P-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="trace:P-bounds">[trace:P-bounds]</a> therefore show that $F$, $G$, and $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(RZ^2)$ are integrable against $t^\beta\,dt$, and that $t^{\beta+1}F(t)$ vanishes at both endpoints.

<a id="trace:weighted"></a>
Differentiating <a href="#trace:F" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="trace:F">[trace:F]</a> and using <a href="#trace:F-evaluation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="trace:F-evaluation">[trace:F-evaluation]</a>–<a href="#trace:G" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="trace:G">[trace:G]</a> gives $$F'(t)=-\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}V}{2t^{3/2}}+\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}P(t),
 \qquad tF'(t)=-\tfrac12F(t)-G(t).$$ Integration by parts, with the endpoint limits just proved, yields $\int_0^\infty t^\beta G\,dt
 =\sigma\int_0^\infty t^\beta F\,dt$. Consequently, integration of <a href="#trace:FG" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="trace:FG">[trace:FG]</a> gives $$\label{trace:weighted}
 \mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(NV)=q c_\sigma\int_0^\infty t^\beta G(t)\,dt
       +c_\sigma\int_0^\infty t^\beta\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(RZ^2)\,dt.$$ It remains to express the first integral as $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(N^q)/q$ plus a nonnegative remainder.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### The noncommutative square

<a id="trace:F0"></a>
For a positive semidefinite matrix $D$, let $$F_0(t,D)=\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}D}{\sqrt t}
       -2\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl((tI+D)^{1/2}-\sqrt t I\bigr).$$ For a scalar eigenvalue $d\geq0$, the corresponding summand is $\int_0^d(t^{-1/2}-(t+y)^{-1/2})\,dy$. Tonelli’s theorem and <a href="#trace:scaling" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="trace:scaling">[trace:scaling]</a> therefore imply $$\label{trace:F0}
 c_\sigma\int_0^\infty t^\beta F_0(t,D)\,dt
     =\frac1p\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(D^p).$$ Now expand the Hilbert–Schmidt square <a id="trace:square"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal D_D(t)
  &:=\bigl\|R^{-1/2}-R^{1/2}(tI+D)^{1/2}\bigr\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
       \notag\\
  &=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl(R^{-1}+R(tI+D)-2(tI+D)^{1/2}\bigr)
       \label{trace:square}\\
  &=G(t)-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(P(t)D)+F_0(t,D).\notag
\end{aligned}$$ No commutation is used: the cross terms cancel adjacent factors $R^{-1/2}R^{1/2}$, and cyclicity changes the remaining quadratic term into $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(R(tI+D))$. All three terms in the last line are integrable by <a href="#trace:P-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="trace:P-bounds">[trace:P-bounds]</a>, <a href="#trace:FG" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="trace:FG">[trace:FG]</a>, and <a href="#trace:F0" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="trace:F0">[trace:F0]</a>. Thus <a id="trace:duality"></a>$$\label{trace:duality}
 c_\sigma\int_0^\infty t^\beta G(t)\,dt
  =\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(ND)-\frac1p\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(D^p)
       +c_\sigma\int_0^\infty t^\beta\mathcal D_D(t)\,dt.$$ When $N\geq0$, choose $D=N^r$. Since $rp=q=1+r$, the first two terms on the right equal $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(N^q)/q$. Substitution into <a href="#trace:weighted" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="trace:weighted">[trace:weighted]</a> proves <a href="#trace:deficit-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="trace:deficit-identity">[trace:deficit-identity]</a> and completes the proof of Theorem <a href="#trace:deficit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="trace:deficit">10</a>.

If $B$ and $V$ commute, the symbol hypothesis implies $V\geq bI$. Simultaneous diagonalization then gives $R(t)=(tI+V)^{-1/2}$, $N=V^\sigma$, and $Z=0$. Both remainders vanish, as required by scalar equality.

<a id="app:paths"></a>
### The regularized field and connecting paths

The finite-interval action argument requires a symmetric matrix path from $K$ to $-K$ whose derivative measures the difference between a matrix field and $AA^{\mathsf T}$. We construct both the field and the path here. These are the constructions of <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[MLT](#ref-MLT), Sections 4–5]; we include their proofs to make the analytic input to the equality argument self-contained. The positivity statement below also identifies the resolvent to which Theorem <a href="#trace:deficit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="trace:deficit">10</a> applies.

Throughout this appendix, $K=\operatorname{diag}(k_1,\ldots,k_n)$ with $k_i>0$, $\kappa=\max_i k_i$, and $\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_n$ denotes the real symmetric matrices. We use $\beta=\sigma-\tfrac12$ and the constant $c_\sigma$ from <a href="#trace:normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="trace:normalization">[trace:normalization]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### A field with a scalar boundary integral

<a id="field:f"></a>
<a id="field:f-derivative"></a>
For $\delta>0$, define on $y\geq0$ $$\label{field:f}
 f_\delta(y)=\frac{c_\sigma}{\pi}\int_0^\infty t^\beta
       \left(\frac1{t+\delta}-\frac1{t+\delta+y}\right)\,dt,$$ and extend to $y<0$ by the tangent line $f_\delta'(0)y$. The resulting function is $C^1$, strictly increasing, and concave. Indeed, for $y\geq0$, $$\label{field:f-derivative}
 f_\delta'(y)=a_\sigma(y+\delta)^{\beta-1},
 \qquad
 a_\sigma=\frac{c_\sigma}{\pi}
       \int_0^\infty\frac{u^\beta}{(1+u)^2}\,du>0.$$ Both integrals converge since $-\tfrac12<\beta<\tfrac12$. The extension allows the functional calculus below even when its matrix argument has negative eigenvalues.

<a id="field:definition"></a>
<a id="field:mu"></a>
Set $$\label{field:definition}
 \begin{split}
 Q_s(B)&=s^2I-2sB+K^2,\\
 M_\delta(B)&=\lim_{R\to\infty}\int_{-R}^{R}
       \bigl(f_\delta(Q_s(B))-f_\delta(s^2)I\bigr)\,ds,
       \qquad B\in\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_n,
 \end{split}$$ where the symmetric cutoff cancels the leading term $-2s f_\delta'(s^2)B$, which is odd in $s$. The scalar counterpart is $$\label{field:mu}
 \mu_\delta(z)=\int_\mathbb R
     \bigl(f_\delta(s^2+z)-f_\delta(s^2)\bigr)\,ds,
       \qquad z\in\mathbb R.$$

<a id="stmt-field-properties"></a>
<div id="field:properties" class="proposition">

<a id="field:primitive"></a>
<a id="field:mu-positive"></a>
<a id="field:direction"></a>
<a id="field:coordinate"></a>
<a id="field:equivariance"></a>
<a id="field:endpoints"></a>
**Proposition 11** (Regularized field). *The limit in <a href="#field:definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="field:definition">[field:definition]</a> exists locally uniformly, and $M_\delta:\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_n\to\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_n$ is locally Lipschitz. There is a $C^1$ function $J_\delta:\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_n\to\mathbb R$, normalized by $J_\delta(0)=0$, such that $$\label{field:primitive}
 dJ_\delta(B)[H]=-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(M_\delta(B)H).$$ The scalar function $\mu_\delta$ is locally Lipschitz and strictly increasing, with $\mu_\delta(0)=0$, and $$\label{field:mu-positive}
       \mu_\delta(z)=(z+\delta)^\sigma-\delta^\sigma
       \qquad(z\geq0).$$ For every real unit vector $e$, and for every standard basis vector $e_i$, respectively, $$\begin{aligned}
 e^{\mathsf T}M_\delta(B)e
  &\leq\mu_\delta\bigl(e^{\mathsf T}K^2e
                         -(e^{\mathsf T}Be)^2\bigr),
       \label{field:direction}\\
 Be_i=ye_i\quad&\Longrightarrow\quad
 M_\delta(B)e_i=\mu_\delta(k_i^2-y^2)e_i.
       \label{field:coordinate}
\end{aligned}$$ For each diagonal sign matrix $S$, $$\label{field:equivariance}
                 M_\delta(SBS)=SM_\delta(B)S.$$ Finally, $$\label{field:endpoints}
 \begin{split}
 J_\delta(-K)-J_\delta(K)
 &=\sum_{i=1}^n\int_{-k_i}^{k_i}\mu_\delta(k_i^2-s^2)\,ds\\
 &\longrightarrow b_\sigma\sum_{i=1}^n k_i^{2\sigma+1}
       \qquad(\delta\downarrow0).
 \end{split}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-field-properties"><summary>Proof of stmt-field-properties</summary>

<a id="field:HS-calculus"></a>
*Proof.* We first justify the matrix integral, including its local Lipschitz dependence. If a scalar function $h$ is Lipschitz on an interval containing the spectra of Hermitian matrices $X,Y$, then $$\label{field:HS-calculus}
 \|h(X)-h(Y)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq\operatorname{Lip}(h)\|X-Y\|_{\mathrm{HS}}.$$ To see this, use eigenbases $e_i$ of $X$ and $v_j$ of $Y$. The mixed matrix entries of the two differences are $(h(\lambda_i)-h(\nu_j))\langle e_i,v_j\rangle$ and $(\lambda_i-\nu_j)\langle e_i,v_j\rangle$; square the scalar Lipschitz inequality and sum over $i,j$.

On a bounded set of $B$’s the spectrum of $E_s(B)=-2sB+K^2$ is contained in $[-a|s|,a|s|]$ for all sufficiently large $|s|$. For $$h_s(y)=f_\delta(s^2+y)-f_\delta(s^2)-f_\delta'(s^2)y,$$ Equation <a href="#field:f-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="field:f-derivative">[field:f-derivative]</a> gives $\sup_{|y|\leq a|s|}|h_s'(y)|=O(|s|^{2\beta-3})$. It follows from <a href="#field:HS-calculus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="field:HS-calculus">[field:HS-calculus]</a> that $$f_\delta(Q_s(B))-f_\delta(s^2)I
      =-2s f_\delta'(s^2)B+T_s(B),$$ where both $\|T_s(B)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}$ and the local Lipschitz constant of $T_s$ are $O(|s|^{2\beta-2})$. For the first bound, include $f_\delta'(s^2)K^2$ in $T_s$; for the second, apply <a href="#field:HS-calculus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="field:HS-calculus">[field:HS-calculus]</a> to $h_s(E_s(B))$ and use $E_s(B_1)-E_s(B_2)=-2s(B_1-B_2)$. Because $2\beta-2<-1$, these remainders are integrable at infinity. The odd term cancels at each symmetric cutoff. On bounded $s$-intervals, <a href="#field:HS-calculus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="field:HS-calculus">[field:HS-calculus]</a> applies directly to $f_\delta$. This proves the asserted convergence and regularity.

For the primitive, choose a scalar antiderivative $g_\delta'=f_\delta$. Trace differentiation gives $d\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(g_\delta(X))[H]=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(f_\delta(X)H)$. This formula follows for polynomials by cyclicity and for $g_\delta$ by approximation in $C^1$ on compact spectral intervals. For $s\ne0$, the function $$B\longmapsto
 \frac{\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(g_\delta(Q_s(B))-g_\delta(K^2))}{2s}
                  +f_\delta(s^2)\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}B$$ has differential $-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}((f_\delta(Q_s(B))-f_\delta(s^2)I)H)$. At $s=0$ that differential is constant in $B$ and also has a primitive. Thus the negative of every finite-cutoff field has zero integral around each closed piecewise smooth curve in $\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_n$. Local uniform convergence passes this property to $-M_\delta$. Path integration, starting at zero, defines $J_\delta$ and proves <a href="#field:primitive" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="field:primitive">[field:primitive]</a>.

For bounded $z$, the integrand in <a href="#field:mu" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="field:mu">[field:mu]</a> and its Lipschitz constant in $z$ are $O(|s|^{2\beta-2})$ at infinity. This proves absolute convergence and local Lipschitz continuity of $\mu_\delta$. Strict increase and $\mu_\delta(0)=0$ follow from the same properties of $f_\delta$. For $z\geq0$, Tonelli’s theorem applied to <a href="#field:f" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="field:f">[field:f]</a> gives $$\mu_\delta(z)=c_\sigma\int_0^\infty t^\beta
       \bigl((t+\delta)^{-1/2}-(t+\delta+z)^{-1/2}\bigr)\,dt.$$ The normalization <a href="#trace:normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="trace:normalization">[trace:normalization]</a>, after scaling, evaluates this as <a href="#field:mu-positive" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="field:mu-positive">[field:mu-positive]</a>.

<a id="field:shift"></a>
Translations in the variable $s$ remain legitimate with a symmetric cutoff. More precisely, for fixed $a,z\in\mathbb R$, $$\label{field:shift}
 \lim_{R\to\infty}\int_{-R}^{R}
   \bigl(f_\delta((s-a)^2+z)-f_\delta(s^2)\bigr)\,ds
          =\mu_\delta(z).$$ Indeed $F_z(s)=f_\delta(s^2+z)$ is even and $F_z'(s)=O(|s|^{2\beta-1})\to0$ at infinity. Pairing the two short intervals produced by a translation bounds the difference between the shifted and unshifted cutoff integrals by $$|a|^2\sup_{R-|a|\leq u\leq R+|a|}|F_z'(u)|,$$ which tends to zero.

Scalar concavity in an eigenbasis of $Q_s(B)$ now gives $$e^{\mathsf T}f_\delta(Q_s(B))e
     \leq f_\delta(e^{\mathsf T}Q_s(B)e).$$ The scalar argument on the right is $(s-e^{\mathsf T}Be)^2+e^{\mathsf T}K^2e-(e^{\mathsf T}Be)^2$. Integration and <a href="#field:shift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="field:shift">[field:shift]</a> prove <a href="#field:direction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="field:direction">[field:direction]</a>. If $Be_i=ye_i$, functional calculus gives equality in the corresponding coordinate, proving <a href="#field:coordinate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="field:coordinate">[field:coordinate]</a>. Since $SK^2S=K^2$, conjugation inside the defining integral proves <a href="#field:equivariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="field:equivariance">[field:equivariance]</a>.

On diagonal matrices, <a href="#field:primitive" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="field:primitive">[field:primitive]</a> and <a href="#field:coordinate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="field:coordinate">[field:coordinate]</a> read $$dJ_\delta(\operatorname{diag}(b_1,\ldots,b_n))
       =-\sum_i\mu_\delta(k_i^2-b_i^2)\,db_i.$$ Integrating from $K$ to $-K$ gives the first equality in <a href="#field:endpoints" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="field:endpoints">[field:endpoints]</a>. For $z\geq0$, $0\leq\mu_\delta(z)\leq z^\sigma$ and $\mu_\delta(z)\to z^\sigma$. Dominated convergence and the substitution $s=k_i u$ give its stated limit. ◻

</details>

The field is defined on all symmetric matrices, but the action argument compares it with a positive matrix $AA^{\mathsf T}$. At such values its directional bound forces positivity of the entire quadratic symbol $Q_s(B)$. This is the condition needed for the trace deficit formula.

<a id="stmt-field-positive"></a>
<div id="field:positive" class="proposition">

<a id="field:N"></a>
**Proposition 12** (Positive values of the field). *If $M=M_\delta(B)\geq0$, then <a id="field:positive-bounds"></a>$$\label{field:positive-bounds}
 Q_s(B)\geq0\quad(s\in\mathbb R),\qquad
             0\leq M\leq\kappa^{2\sigma}I.$$ Set $V=K^2-B^2+\delta I$ and use $B,V$ in <a href="#trace:resolvents" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="trace:resolvents">[trace:resolvents]</a> and <a href="#trace:N" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="trace:N">[trace:N]</a>. Then the symbol $(sI-B)^2+V=Q_s(B)+\delta I$ is bounded below by $\delta I$, and $$\label{field:N}
                         N=M+\delta^\sigma I.$$ In particular, the hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#trace:deficit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="trace:deficit">10</a> hold.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-field-positive"><summary>Proof of stmt-field-positive</summary>

*Proof.* By <a href="#field:direction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="field:direction">[field:direction]</a>, positivity of $M$ and strict increase of $\mu_\delta$ imply $e^{\mathsf T}K^2e\geq(e^{\mathsf T}Be)^2$ for every real unit vector $e$. Completing the scalar square gives $$e^{\mathsf T}Q_s(B)e=(s-e^{\mathsf T}Be)^2
                +e^{\mathsf T}K^2e-(e^{\mathsf T}Be)^2\geq0.$$ Thus $Q_s(B)\geq0$, also as a Hermitian matrix on $\mathbb C^n$. Moreover, <a href="#field:direction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="field:direction">[field:direction]</a> gives $M\leq\mu_\delta(\kappa^2)I\leq\kappa^{2\sigma}I$, using $(a+b)^\sigma\leq a^\sigma+b^\sigma$.

Write $Y_s=(sI-B)^2$. Diagonalization of $B$ and <a href="#field:shift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="field:shift">[field:shift]</a> allow replacement of the scalar subtraction $f_\delta(s^2)I$ in <a href="#field:definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="field:definition">[field:definition]</a> by $f_\delta(Y_s)$. Since both $Q_s(B)$ and $Y_s$ are positive, their difference under $f_\delta$ is, by <a href="#field:f" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="field:f">[field:f]</a>, $$\frac{c_\sigma}{\pi}\int_0^\infty t^\beta
 \left((Y_s+(t+\delta)I)^{-1}
          -(Q_s(B)+(t+\delta)I)^{-1}\right)\,dt.$$ For fixed $B,\delta$, both matrices being inverted are bounded below by $c(1+t+s^2)I$. Their difference is $K^2-B^2$, so the resolvent identity bounds the norm of the integrand by $C t^\beta(1+t+s^2)^{-2}$. Integrating first in $s$ yields $C't^\beta(1+t)^{-3/2}$, which is integrable on $(0,\infty)$. Fubini’s theorem is therefore applicable and gives $$M=c_\sigma\int_0^\infty t^\beta
        \bigl((t+\delta)^{-1/2}I-R(t)\bigr)\,dt.$$ Subtracting this expression from <a href="#trace:N" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="trace:N">[trace:N]</a> and scaling <a href="#trace:normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="trace:normalization">[trace:normalization]</a> gives <a href="#field:N" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="field:N">[field:N]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### Choosing the lower triangular coefficient matrix

Let $\mathcal L_n$ be the real lower triangular $n\times n$ matrices. Their dimension equals that of $\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_n$. This dimension match allows us to prescribe a symmetric terminal value by choosing $C\in\mathcal L_n$. At a simple initial problem there is one choice up to signs of the rows. Continuation preserves the parity of this number, and hence produces the required terminal value.

<a id="stmt-path-connecting"></a>
<div id="path:connecting" class="proposition">

<a id="path:ode"></a>
<a id="path:bounds"></a>
**Proposition 13** (Connecting path). *Let $\Omega=(x_-,x_+)$ be bounded and let $U\in H^1_0(\Omega;\mathbb R^{n\times d})$, where $n,d\geq1$, satisfy $$\int_\Omega UU^{\mathsf T}\,dx=I_n.$$ For every $\delta,\varepsilon>0$ there are $C_\varepsilon\in\mathcal L_n$ and $B_\varepsilon\in C^1(\overline\Omega;\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_n)$ such that, with $A_\varepsilon=C_\varepsilon U$, $$\label{path:ode}
 \varepsilon B_\varepsilon'
       =M_\delta(B_\varepsilon)-A_\varepsilon A_\varepsilon^{\mathsf T},
 \qquad B_\varepsilon(x_-)=K,\quad B_\varepsilon(x_+)=-K.$$ They satisfy $$\label{path:bounds}
 \sup_{x\in\overline\Omega}\|B_\varepsilon(x)\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}\leq\kappa,
 \qquad
 \sup_{0<\varepsilon\leq1}\|C_\varepsilon\|_{\mathrm{HS}}<\infty,$$ where the second bound is for fixed $\delta,K,\Omega$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-path-connecting"><summary>Proof of stmt-path-connecting</summary>

*Proof.* Use the continuous representative of $U$. Choose a smooth, nonnegative, compactly supported cutoff $\chi$ on $\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_n$, equal to one on $\{B:\|B\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}\leq\kappa\}$ and invariant under conjugation by diagonal sign matrices. Averaging a cutoff over the finite sign group produces such a function. The truncated field $\widehat M=\chi M_\delta$ is bounded and globally Lipschitz.

<a id="path:homotopy"></a>
<a id="path:equivariance"></a>
Fix $\varepsilon>0$. For $C\in\mathcal L_n$ and $\theta\in[0,1]$, the equation $$\label{path:homotopy}
 \varepsilon B'=\theta\widehat M(B)-(CU)(CU)^{\mathsf T},
                         \qquad B(x_-)=K$$ has a unique $C^1$ solution on $\overline\Omega$. Define the continuous endpoint map $$\Phi(C,\theta)=B(x_+)+K\in\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_n.$$ Let $\mathcal G$ be the group of diagonal sign matrices, acting by $C\mapsto SC$ on $\mathcal L_n$ and by conjugation on $\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_n$. Equivariance of the field and uniqueness of the ODE give $$\label{path:equivariance}
                       \Phi(SC,\theta)=S\Phi(C,\theta)S.$$

<a id="path:C-bound"></a>
We record the three facts needed for continuation: compactness of the zero set, freeness of the sign action there, and one regular orbit of zeros at $\theta=0$. At a zero, integration of <a href="#path:homotopy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="path:homotopy">[path:homotopy]</a> gives <a id="path:C-identity"></a>$$\label{path:C-identity}
       CC^{\mathsf T}=2\varepsilon K
                       +\theta\int_\Omega\widehat M(B(x))\,dx.$$ Consequently all zeros lie in a bounded set, and their joint set in $\mathcal L_n\times[0,1]$ is compact. For $0<\varepsilon\leq1$, this calculation gives the uniform bound $$\label{path:C-bound}
 \|C\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\leq2\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}K
       +n|\Omega|\sup_{B\in\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_n}\|\widehat M(B)\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}.$$

No row of $C$ can vanish at a zero. Otherwise the reflection changing only row $i$ would fix $C$; ODE uniqueness and <a href="#path:equivariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="path:equivariance">[path:equivariance]</a> would force $Be_i=y e_i$ throughout the interval. Equation <a href="#field:coordinate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="field:coordinate">[field:coordinate]</a> would then give $$\varepsilon y'=\theta\chi(B(x))\mu_\delta(k_i^2-y^2),
                         \qquad y(x_-)=k_i.$$ The coefficient $\chi(B(x))$ is continuous and the right side is locally Lipschitz in $y$. Uniqueness forces $y\equiv k_i$, contradicting $y(x_+)=-k_i$. Thus the action of $\mathcal G$ on the zero set is free.

<a id="path:initial-map"></a>
At $\theta=0$ one has $$\label{path:initial-map}
                     \Phi(C,0)=2K-\varepsilon^{-1}CC^{\mathsf T}.$$ Its lower triangular zeros are exactly $C=\operatorname{diag}(d_1,\ldots,d_n)$ with $d_i=\pm\sqrt{2\varepsilon k_i}$. Indeed, the first row of $CC^{\mathsf T}=2\varepsilon K$ fixes $|d_1|$ and forces all other entries of the first column to vanish, and induction does the same for subsequent columns. These $2^n$ zeros form one sign orbit. At each, the derivative in a lower triangular direction $H$ has diagonal entries $-2\varepsilon^{-1}d_i H_{ii}$ and lower off-diagonal entries $-\varepsilon^{-1}d_j H_{ij}$ for $i>j$. It is therefore an isomorphism $\mathcal L_n\to\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_n$.

<a id="path:gap-set"></a>
We next justify the continuation using only smooth approximation and the regular-value argument for parity <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Milnor1965](#ref-Milnor1965), Sections 2–4]. Suppose there were no zero at $\theta=1$. Compactness and freeness give a bounded invariant open set $O\subset\mathcal L_n$ containing the projection of every zero, whose closure lies where all rows are nonzero. There is a positive lower bound for $\|\Phi\|_{\mathrm{HS}}$ on $$\label{path:gap-set}
       (\partial O\times[0,1])\ \cup\ (\overline O\times\{1\}).$$ Reparametrize $\theta$ by a smooth map equal to zero near zero and equal to one at one. The resulting map $F$ agrees with <a href="#path:initial-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="path:initial-map">[path:initial-map]</a> on an initial collar and retains the gap on <a href="#path:gap-set" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="path:gap-set">[path:gap-set]</a>.

Extend $F$ constantly beyond the two ends of the parameter interval, smooth by convolution, and splice to <a href="#path:initial-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="path:initial-map">[path:initial-map]</a> in a smaller initial collar. Averaging the approximation $\Psi_0$ in the form $$\Psi(C,\theta)=\frac1{|\mathcal G|}
                  \sum_{S\in\mathcal G}S\Psi_0(SC,\theta)S$$ gives a smooth equivariant approximation $\Psi$ that still equals the initial map on that collar. It can be made uniformly close enough on $\overline O\times[0,1]$ to preserve the positive gap.

Freeness permits an equivariant perturbation with arbitrary target directions. At each $C_0\in\overline O$, choose a ball whose distinct sign translates are disjoint, and a smooth bump $b$ supported in this ball with $b(C_0)=1$. For $H\in\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_n$ the map $$V_H(C)=\sum_{S\in\mathcal G}b(SC)SHS$$ is equivariant and satisfies $V_H(C_0)=H$. Choose a basis of target matrices and then a finite collection of such neighborhoods covering $\overline O$. This gives finitely many smooth equivariant maps $V_1,\ldots,V_N$ whose values span $\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_n$ at every point of $\overline O$.

Let $h\geq0$ be smooth, vanish near zero, and be positive outside the collar where $\Psi$ is the initial map. Choose it so that every zero of $h$ lies within that collar, and set $$\Psi_\alpha(C,\theta)
           =\Psi(C,\theta)+h(\theta)\sum_{j=1}^{N}\alpha_jV_j(C).$$ For $\alpha$ in a sufficiently small open ball, the gap on <a href="#path:gap-set" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="path:gap-set">[path:gap-set]</a> persists. At a zero of this family with $0<\theta<1$, its full differential in $(C,\theta,\alpha)$ is onto: when $h(\theta)>0$ the parameter directions span the target, and when $h(\theta)=0$ the derivative in $C$ is the isomorphism computed for <a href="#path:initial-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="path:initial-map">[path:initial-map]</a>. Its interior zero set is consequently a smooth manifold. Sard’s theorem <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Sard1942](#ref-Sard1942)], applied to projection of this manifold onto the parameter ball, gives arbitrarily small $\alpha$ for which zero is a regular value of $(C,\theta)\mapsto\Psi_\alpha(C,\theta)$. For completeness, if the full derivative is $(L_1,L_2)$ in the $(C,\theta)$ and $\alpha$ directions, regularity of that projection means that each $w$ admits $v$ with $L_1v+L_2w=0$. Hence $\operatorname{ran}L_2\subset
\operatorname{ran}L_1$, and surjectivity of $(L_1,L_2)$ implies surjectivity of $L_1$.

For such an $\alpha$, the zero set in $O\times[0,1]$ is a compact smooth one-dimensional manifold with boundary. The gap excludes boundary zeros except at $\theta=0$, where the initial collar gives exactly the $2^n$ regular zeros above. The free sign action has a quotient that is again a compact one-dimensional manifold with boundary: a neighborhood disjoint from its nontrivial translates supplies the local interval or half-interval chart. All $2^n$ boundary points form one orbit, so the quotient has exactly one boundary point. This is impossible, since a compact one-dimensional manifold is a finite union of circles and closed intervals. Therefore the original map $\Phi(\cdot,1)$ has a zero.

It remains to remove the cutoff. For the path at this zero and any fixed real unit vector $e$, put $y=e^{\mathsf T}Be$. Whenever $|y|>\kappa$, Equation <a href="#field:direction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="field:direction">[field:direction]</a> yields $$e^{\mathsf T}M_\delta(B)e
       \leq\mu_\delta(e^{\mathsf T}K^2e-y^2)<0.$$ Since $\chi\geq0$ and $(CU)(CU)^{\mathsf T}\geq0$, the ODE implies $y'\leq0$ there. Both endpoint values lie in $[-\kappa,\kappa]$. An excursion above $\kappa$ cannot start from $\kappa$ while $y$ is nonincreasing; an excursion below $-\kappa$ cannot return to $-\kappa$ while $y$ is nonincreasing. The two endpoint conditions therefore rule out both excursions. This holds for every $e$, so $\|B(x)\|_{\mathop{\mathrm{op}}}\leq\kappa$. Consequently $\chi(B)=1$, giving <a href="#path:ode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="path:ode">[path:ode]</a>, and <a href="#path:C-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="path:C-bound">[path:C-bound]</a> supplies the remaining assertion in <a href="#path:bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="path:bounds">[path:bounds]</a>. ◻

</details>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The number of rows can grow with the number of negative eigenfunctions, but the number of physical channels remains $m$. After fixing the first $h$ rows, the identity $K_{j}^{2} ≈ B_{j}^{2} + (A_{j}A_{j}^{T})^{r}$ bounds the operator norm in the remaining rows. The rank bound converts it into a Hilbert–Schmidt bound, which tends to zero as first $j → ∞$ and then $h → ∞$. The drawing is schematic; all matrices are padded by zero rows in $ℋ$.",
      "figure_id": "comp:tail-figure",
      "pdf_page": 14,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "comp:tail-figure",
          "exact_quote": "The number of rows can grow with the number of negative\n eigenfunctions, but the number of physical channels remains $m$.\n After fixing the first $h$ rows, the identity\n $K_j^2\\approx B_j^2+(A_jA_j^T)^r$ bounds the operator norm in the\n rem",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\\small,>=Latex]\n  \\fill[black!5] (0,2.05) rectangle (2.7,3.65);\n  \\fill[blue!7] (0,0) rectangle (2.7,2.05);\n  \\draw[thick] (0,0) rectangle (2.7,3.65);\n  \\draw[thick] (0,2.05)--(2.7,2.05);\n  \\draw[<->] (0,4.05)--node[above]{$m$ columns, fixed}(2.7,4.05);\n  \\node at (1.35,2.83) {$P_hA_j$};\n  \\node at (1.35,1.28) {$Q_hA_j$};\n  \\node at (1.35,0.55) {$\\vdots$};\n  \\node[anchor=east,align=right] at (-0.2,2.83)\n       {first $h$\\\\rows};\n  \\node[anchor=east,align=right] at (-0.2,1.08)\n       {all remaining\\\\rows};\n  \\draw[->] (2.9,2.83)--(3.6,2.83);\n  \\node[anchor=west,align=left] at (3.75,2.83)\n       {Finite-dimensional convergence\\\\for each fixed $h$};\n  \\draw[->] (2.9,1.08)--(3.6,1.08);\n  \\node[anchor=west,align=left] at (3.75,1.08)\n       {$\\operatorname{rank}(Q_hA_j)\\leq m$\\\\[4pt]\n        $\\|Q_hA_j\\|_{\\HS}^2\\leq m(s_h+e_j)^{1/r}$};\n \\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Schematic diagram showing a tall rectangular matrix partitioned horizontally. Above the rectangle is a two-sided horizontal arrow labeled \"$m\\text{ columns, fixed}$\". To the left, the top partition is labeled \"first $h$ rows\" and contains the expression \"$P_h A_j$\"; a horizontal arrow points from this block to the text \"Finite-dimensional convergence for each fixed $h$\". The bottom partition is labeled to the left as \"all remaining rows\" and contains \"$Q_h A_j$\" followed by vertical dots ($\\vdots$); a curly bracket to its right groups the inequalities \"$\\operatorname{rank}(Q_h A_j) \\le m$\" and \"$\\|Q_h A_j\\|_{\\mathrm{HS}}^2 \\le m(s_h + e_j)^{1/r}$\"."
    }
  ],
